\displaystyle Q=\gamma m\lambda^{2}D^{2}+\gamma\lambda^{2}\kappa D-\frac{\gamma\lambda^{2}}{1-m}\mathbb{E}D(mD+\kappa)H+\frac{m}{1-m},

(150)

\displaystyle H=(\tilde{V}-\mu\lambda D+\gamma\lambda^{2}D^{2}-\gamma\lambda D^{3})^{-1}.

(151)

For a given value of the parameter
m
, the saddle point equations require
\tilde{V}=\tilde{V}(m)
to be the solution of the implicit equation

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}H

\displaystyle=1-m.

(152)

Using this implicit solution,
H
is a function
H(m)
and
Q=Q(m,\kappa)
. Let
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
and
X^{*}\sim P_{X}
. The saddle point equations over the order parameters
(m,\kappa)
read

\displaystyle m

\displaystyle=1-{\rm mmse}(\hat{m}),

(153)

\displaystyle\kappa

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}DQH,

(154)

where
{\rm mmse}(\hat{m})
is the same function (
146
) as before and

\displaystyle\hat{m}

\displaystyle=\hat{m}(m,\kappa)=\gamma\lambda^{2}\mathbb{E}H\Big(\frac{mD^{2}+\kappa D}{1-m}+D^{2}Q\Big)+\mu\lambda^{2}m.

(155)

Recall that the replica prediction for the MMSE is (
100
). In the regime
\lambda\to\infty
we thus necessarily have
m\to 1^{-}
. Since the solution
(m,\kappa)
of the replica saddle point equations yields the MMSE (
100
) which must be at least as good as the AMP MSE (
143
) then
m\geq\Delta_{*}
. Thus from (
148
) we deduce

\displaystyle 1-m=O\Big(\exp\Big(-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}(1+o_{\lambda}(1))\Big)\Big).

(156)

The support of the density of
D
is bounded, therefore from (
151
) it is then clear that for (
152
) to be verified under the scaling (
156
) in the large
\lambda
limit, the solution
\tilde{V}
of (
152
) must verify

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda^{2}}{\tilde{V}}=o_{\lambda}(1).

(157)

Thus from (
152
) we obtain

\displaystyle(1-m)\tilde{V}=\mathbb{E}\Big(1+\frac{\gamma\lambda D^{2}(\lambda-D)-\mu\lambda D}{\tilde{V}}\Big)^{-1}=1+o_{\lambda}(1)

(158)

from which we deduce using (
156
) that

\displaystyle\tilde{V}=\Theta\Big(\frac{1}{1-m}\Big)=\Omega\Big(\exp\Big(\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}(1+o_{\lambda}(1))\Big)\Big).

(159)

This also implies that in the limit of large SNR,
H
becomes deterministic:

\displaystyle H=\tilde{V}^{-1}+O\Big(\frac{\lambda^{2}}{\tilde{V}^{2}}\Big).

(160)

This equality means that
H
can be written as
\tilde{V}^{-1}
plus a possibly random term dependent of
D
, which can be bounded by a non-random constant of order
O(\lambda^{2}/\tilde{V}^{2})
. Similarly for
Q
: using that
\kappa
is bounded (recall that it is the limit of the expectation of (
60
)), (
160
) and (
158
), we get the following deterministic scaling in the large SNR regime:

\displaystyle Q=\frac{m}{1-m}+O(\lambda^{2}).

(161)

Using all these scalings together with the fact that
\mathbb{E}D=0
and
\kappa
is bounded (actually it can now be seen from the
(\hat{\kappa})
-equation of Section
7.3
that
\kappa=o_{\lambda}(1)
) we reach, using
\mathbb{E}D^{2}=1
and (
157
), (
158
),

\displaystyle\hat{m}

\displaystyle=\gamma\lambda^{2}\mathbb{E}H\Big(\frac{mD^{2}+\kappa D}{1-m}+D^{2}Q\Big)+\mu\lambda^{2}m

\displaystyle=\gamma\lambda^{2}\Big(\tilde{V}^{-1}+O\Big(\frac{\lambda^{2}}{\tilde{V}^{2}}\Big)\Big)\Big(\frac{2m}{1-m}+O(\lambda^{2})\Big)+\mu\lambda^{2}m

\displaystyle=\gamma\lambda^{2}\Big(\frac{2m}{\tilde{V}(1-m)}+O\Big(\frac{\lambda^{2}}{\tilde{V}}\Big)+O\Big(\frac{\lambda^{2}}{\tilde{V}}\times\frac{1}{\tilde{V}(1-m)}\Big)+O\Big(\frac{\lambda^{4}}{\tilde{V}^{2}}\Big)\Big)+\mu\lambda^{2}m

\displaystyle=\gamma\lambda^{2}(2m+o_{\lambda}(1))+\mu\lambda^{2}m

\displaystyle=\lambda^{2}(2\gamma+\mu)(1+o_{\lambda}(1))

(162)

where also used
m=1+o_{\lambda}(1)
, see (
156
). Recall
m=1-{\rm mmse}(\hat{m})
as well as the scaling (
147
). So we have

\displaystyle m=1-\exp\Big(-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}(2\gamma+\mu)(1+o_{\lambda}(1))\Big).

(163)

By comparing with (
148
) we see that
m\neq\Delta_{*}
. Moreover, since
m
is the Bayes-optimal overlap, it has to be the case that
m\geq\Delta_{*}
, namely,
2\gamma+\mu\geq 1
. From (
32
) it can be verified that
2\gamma+\mu>1
strictly for
\mu<1
. Equality holds for the pure Wigner case
(\mu=1,\gamma=0)
, as expected. This ends the proof that the MMSE (
100
) is asympotically in
\lambda
strictly exponentially smaller than the MSE of AMP with one-term memory (
143
) whenever
\mu<1,\gamma>0
.

8.3
What is actually doing this sub-optimal AMP?

Mismatched estimation with Gaussian likelihood

In the same spirit as
[
14
]
, we study here a mismatched estimation where the statistician assumes the noise to be Gaussian, thus a wrong likelihood, whereas the noise is drawn from the quartic ensemble with potential (
28
).
In the same way as we did for the quartic potential, the mismatched posterior associated to (
17
) is written as

\displaystyle d\bar{P}_{X|Y}(\mathbf{x}\mid\mathbf{Y})=\frac{1}{\bar{\mathcal{Z}}(\mathbf{Y})}dP_{X}(\mathbf{x})\exp\Big(\frac{\lambda}{2}{\rm Tr}\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{x}{\mathbf{x}}^{\intercal}-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{4N}\|\mathbf{x}\|^{4}\Big)

(164)

where we have re-absorbed
\mathbf{x}
-independent terms in the normalization. The corresponding log-partition function is

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\ln\bar{\mathcal{Z}}(\mathbf{Y}).

(165)

Notice that we have barred some quantities to distinguish them from their Bayes-optimal analogues. We further stress that, with Gaussian likelihood, the spin-glass model that arises already contains only two body interactions.

We aim at
approximating
(
165
). Indeed, we are going to perform a replica symmetric computation, which has no a-priori reasons to be exact as we are not anymore in the Bayesian-optimal setting
[
20
]
(nor the mismatched posterior is log-concave
[
21
]
, see also
[
31
]
as a counter-example). We denote jointly
\boldsymbol{\tau}=(v_{1},q_{01})
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
their Fourier conjugates. The partition function can then be expressed using deltas to fix the
\boldsymbol{\tau}
parameters and expanding
\mathbf{Y}
as in (
17
). Up to irrelevant constants it reads

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{Z}}(\mathbf{Y})=\int dP_{X}(\mathbf{x})d\boldsymbol{\tau}d\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\exp\big(-\bar{H}_{N}(\boldsymbol{\tau},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}},\mathbf{x};\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{Z})\big)

(166)

where

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bar{H}_{N}(\boldsymbol{\tau},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}},\mathbf{x};\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{Z}):=N\bar{h}(\boldsymbol{\tau},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}})+\mathbf{x}^{\intercal}\bar{\mathbf{J}}(\boldsymbol{\tau},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}},\mathbf{Z})\mathbf{x}+\hat{q}_{01}\mathbf{x}^{\intercal}\mathbf{x}_{0}\end{split}

(167)

and

\displaystyle\bar{h}(\boldsymbol{\tau},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}}):=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{4}v_{1}^{2}-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}q_{01}^{2}-q_{01}\hat{q}_{01}-\frac{v_{1}\hat{v}_{1}}{2},

(168)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbf{J}}(\boldsymbol{\tau},\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}},\mathbf{Z}):=\frac{\hat{v}_{1}}{2}I_{N}-\frac{\lambda}{2}\mathbf{Z}.

(169)

While replicating we will need as before to fix the entire overlap structure (and not only
q_{01}
), i.e.,
(N\mathbf{q})_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}=Nq_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}=\mathbf{x}_{\ell}^{\intercal}\mathbf{x}_{\ell^{\prime}}
, the diagonal elements being denoted as
v_{\ell}
. As usual, we also introduce the corresponding Fourier conjugates
\hat{\mathbf{q}}
. The expected replicated partition function then reads as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{n}

\displaystyle=\int d\mathbf{q}d\hat{\mathbf{q}}\exp N\Big(\sum_{\ell\leq n}\Big(\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}q_{0\ell}^{2}-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{4}v_{\ell}^{2}+\frac{v_{\ell}\hat{v}_{\ell}}{2}\Big)+\sum_{0\leq\ell<\ell^{\prime}\leq n}\hat{q}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}q_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\int\prod_{\ell=0}^{n}dP_{X}(\mathbf{x}_{\ell})\exp\Big(-\sum_{0\leq\ell<\ell^{\prime}\leq n}\hat{q}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}\mathbf{x}^{\intercal}_{\ell}\mathbf{x}_{\ell^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell\leq n}\hat{v}_{\ell}\|\mathbf{x}_{\ell}\|^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{O}}\exp\Big(\frac{\lambda}{2}{\rm Tr}\mathbf{O}\mathbf{D}\mathbf{O}^{\intercal}\sum_{\ell\leq n}\mathbf{x}_{\ell}\mathbf{x}_{\ell}^{\intercal}\Big).

(170)

In the last line we recognize a rank-
n
(standard) spherical integral, see Section
6.2.1
and
[
58
]
. Recall the spectrum is deterministic with empirical law tending weakly to
\rho
. Hence we can use the results from Section
6.2.3
, with the difference that
\mathbf{C}=I_{n}\frac{\lambda}{2}D
is virtually a scalar random variable, and thus w.l.o.g. we can also assume
\mathbf{q}
, and thus
\tilde{\mathbf{q}}
to be diagonal in (
43
). If we aim for a replica symmetric ansatz

\displaystyle\text{Replica Symmetric Ansatz: }\begin{cases}v_{\ell}=v,\quad\hat{v}_{\ell}=\hat{v}\\
q_{0\ell}=m,\quad\hat{q}_{0\ell}=-\hat{m}\\
q_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}=q,\quad\hat{q}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}=-\hat{q}\;(\ell\neq\ell^{\prime})\end{cases}

(171)

then
\mathbf{q}
has a non degenerate eigenvalue
v+(n-1)q
and
n-1
degenerate eigenvalues
v-q
. Within this ansatz we can thus replace the mentioned spherical integral with

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{O}}\exp\Big(\frac{\lambda}{2}{\rm Tr}\mathbf{O}\mathbf{D}\mathbf{O}^{\intercal}\sum_{\ell\leq n}\mathbf{x}_{\ell}\mathbf{x}_{\ell}^{\intercal}\Big)=\exp N\Big((n-1)I_{D}(v-q)+I_{D}(v-q+nq)\Big)\\
=\exp Nn\Big(I_{D}(v-q)+I^{\prime}_{D}(v-q)q+O(n)\Big)

(172)

as done in
[
94
]
, where
I_{D}(\cdot)
are rank-one spherical integrals. The rest can be treated exactly as in Section
7.2
, yielding

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}\ln\bar{\mathcal{Z}}(\mathbf{Y})=\text{extr}\Big\{\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}m^{2}-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{4}v^{2}+\frac{\hat{v}v}{2}-\hat{m}m+\frac{\hat{q}q}{2}+I_{D}(v-q)

\displaystyle\qquad+qI^{\prime}_{D}(v-q)+\mathbb{E}\ln\int dP_{X}(x)\exp\Big(\sqrt{\hat{q}}Zx-\frac{\hat{q}+\hat{v}}{2}x^{2}+\hat{m}X_{0}x\Big)\Big\}

(173)

where extremization is intended over
m,\hat{m},q,\hat{q},v,\hat{v}
. With the same notation for the local measure (
82
), the fixed point equations read

\displaystyle(m)\qquad\hat{m}=\lambda^{2}m

(174)

\displaystyle(\hat{m})\qquad m=\mathbb{E}X_{0}\langle X\rangle_{\hat{m},\hat{q},\hat{v}}

(175)

\displaystyle(q)\qquad\hat{q}=2qI_{D}^{\prime\prime}(v-q)

(176)

\displaystyle(\hat{q})\qquad q=\mathbb{E}\langle X\rangle^{2}_{\hat{m},\hat{q},\hat{v}}

(177)

\displaystyle(v)\qquad\hat{v}=\lambda^{2}v-2I_{D}^{\prime}(v-q)-2qI_{D}^{\prime\prime}(v-q)

(178)

\displaystyle(\hat{v})\qquad v=\mathbb{E}\langle X^{2}\rangle_{\hat{m},\hat{q},\hat{v}}.

(179)

The computation above follows the same lines as that in
[
94
]
, with the only difference being the presence of a planted signal. In case of Gaussian likelihood, the term arising from the spike though is easily tractable, as well as the term containing the fourth norm of the estimator (see (
164
)). This suggests that the AMP algorithm designed in
[
45
]
, whose aim was to make the results in
[
94
,
92
]
rigorous, has to match the performance predicted by our replica computation, measured by the MSE

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{2N^{2}}\mathbb{E}\|{\mathbf{X}}^{*}{\mathbf{X}}^{*\intercal}-\bar{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{X}}^{*}{\mathbf{X}}^{*\intercal}\mid{\mathbf{Y}}]\|_{\rm F}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}(1-2m^{2}+q^{2}).

(180)

in the large
N
limit, where the
\bar{\mathbb{E}}
denotes the expectation w.r.t. (
164
), and
m
and
q
solve (
174
)–(
179
).

An alternative to (
174
)–(
179
), which turns out to be more practical from the numerical point of view, can be obtained by keeping
\mathbf{q}
as it is, without diagonalizing it. In this case one needs the entire formula (
43
), with
\tilde{\mathbf{q}}
having the same RS structure as
\mathbf{q}
, in a similar fashion as that of Section
6.2.3
. The spherical integral then takes the form (up to constants)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{O}}\exp\Big(\frac{\lambda}{2}{\rm Tr}\mathbf{O}\mathbf{D}\mathbf{O}^{\intercal}\sum_{\ell\leq n}\mathbf{x}_{\ell}\mathbf{x}_{\ell}^{\intercal}\Big)\propto\exp\Big(Nn\,\text{extr}\Big\{\frac{v\tilde{v}-q\tilde{q}}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\ln(\tilde{v}-\tilde{q}-\lambda D)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{\tilde{q}}{2}\mathbb{E}(\tilde{v}-\tilde{q}-\lambda D)^{-1}-\frac{1}{2}\ln(v-q)-\frac{q}{2(v-q)}+O(n)\Big\}\Big)

(181)

where extremization is w.r.t. the tilded variables only, for now. Consequently, the free entropy rewrites as follows

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}\ln\bar{\mathcal{Z}}(\mathbf{Y})=\text{extr}\Big\{\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}m^{2}-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{4}v^{2}+\frac{(\hat{v}+\tilde{v})v}{2}-\hat{m}m+\frac{(\hat{q}-\tilde{q})q}{2}

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\ln(\tilde{v}-\tilde{q}-\lambda D)-\frac{\tilde{q}}{2}\mathbb{E}(\tilde{v}-\tilde{q}-\lambda D)^{-1}-\frac{1}{2}\ln(v-q)-\frac{q}{2(v-q)}

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}\ln\int dP_{X}(x)\exp\Big(\sqrt{\hat{q}}Zx-\frac{\hat{q}+\hat{v}}{2}x^{2}+\hat{m}X_{0}x\Big)\Big\}.

(182)

Here instead, extremization is intended over the tilded and hatted variables, together with
m,q,v
.

The fixed point equations are

\displaystyle(m)\qquad\hat{m}=\lambda^{2}m

(183)

\displaystyle(\hat{m})\qquad m=\mathbb{E}X_{0}\langle X\rangle_{\hat{m},\hat{q},\hat{v}}

(184)

\displaystyle(q)\qquad\hat{q}-\tilde{q}=\frac{q}{(v-q)^{2}}

(185)

\displaystyle(\hat{q})\qquad q=\mathbb{E}\langle X\rangle^{2}_{\hat{m},\hat{q},\hat{v}}

(186)

\displaystyle(\tilde{q})\qquad q=-\tilde{q}\mathbb{E}(\tilde{v}-\tilde{q}-\lambda D)^{-2}

(187)

\displaystyle(v)\qquad\hat{v}+\tilde{v}-\lambda^{2}v-\frac{1}{v-q}+\frac{q}{(v-q)^{2}}=0

(188)

\displaystyle(\hat{v})\qquad v=\mathbb{E}\langle X^{2}\rangle_{\hat{m},\hat{q},\hat{v}}

(189)

\displaystyle(\tilde{v})\qquad v-\mathbb{E}(\tilde{v}-\tilde{q}-\lambda D)^{-1}+\tilde{q}\mathbb{E}(\tilde{v}-\tilde{q}-\lambda D)^{-2}=0.

(190)

Plugging
(\tilde{q})
into
(\tilde{v})
we readily see that

\displaystyle v-q=\mathbb{E}(\tilde{V}-\lambda D)^{-1}

(191)

that works as an equation for
\tilde{V}:=\tilde{v}-\tilde{q}
as a function of
v,q
. Analogously, we can plug
(q)
into
(v)
obtaining

\displaystyle\hat{v}+\hat{q}=\lambda^{2}v+\frac{1}{v-q}-\tilde{V}

(192)

that determines
\hat{v}+\hat{q}
as a function of
v
and
q
, thanks to the above equation for
\tilde{V}
.
Finally, from
(\tilde{q})
and
(q)
we have respectively

\displaystyle\tilde{q}=-\frac{q}{\mathbb{E}(\tilde{V}-\lambda D)^{-2}}

(193)

\displaystyle\hat{q}=\frac{q}{(v-q)^{2}}+\tilde{q}.

(194)

Notice that, being in a mismatched setting, there cannot be any simplifications due to the Nishimori identities.

It is not difficult to verify a posteriori that the systems (
174
)–(
179
) and (
183
)–(
190
) are equivalent. The extremization over the tilded variables has indeed the purpose of reproducing
I_{D}
and its derivatives. From (
191
) one can infer

\displaystyle\tilde{V}=R_{\lambda\mathbf{D}}(v-q)+\frac{1}{v-q}

(195)

where
R_{\lambda\mathbf{D}}
denotes the R-transform of
\lambda\mathbf{D}
, and deriving both sides w.r.t.
v
one also has

\displaystyle\tilde{V}^{\prime}=-\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}(\tilde{V}-\lambda D)^{-2}}=R^{\prime}_{\lambda\mathbf{D}}(v-q)-\frac{1}{(v-q)^{2}}.

(196)

Therefore, from (
193
)

\displaystyle\tilde{q}=qR^{\prime}_{\lambda\mathbf{D}}(v-q)-\frac{q}{(v-q)^{2}}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\hat{q}=qR^{\prime}_{\lambda\mathbf{D}}(v-q),

(197)

and from (
192
)

\displaystyle\hat{v}+\hat{q}=\lambda^{2}v-R_{\lambda\mathbf{D}}(v-q),

(198)

both in perfect agreement with (
176
) and (
178
), as long as
R_{\lambda\mathbf{D}}=2I_{D}^{\prime}

[
58
]
.

Figure 4:
Comparison between the fixed point of the AMP algorithm in
[
45
]
and that obtained via the replica computation (cf. (
183
)–(
190
)), for i.i.d. Rademacher distributed
(X_{i}^{*})_{i}
. The agreement between these two fixed points is excellent when the SNR is between 2 and 3.

The system of fixed point equations (
183
)–(
190
) can be solved numerically as follows:
(i)
initialize
m=m_{0}
,
q=q_{0}
,
v=v_{0}
(the latter being identically
1
if we use a Rademacher prior);
(ii)

solve (
191
) for
\tilde{V}
;
(iii)
compute
\hat{q}
,
\tilde{q}
,
\hat{m}
and
\hat{v}+\hat{q}
from (
194
), (
193
), (
183
) and (
192
) respectively;
(iv)
update the values of
m,q,v
through
(\hat{m})
,
(\hat{q})
and
(\hat{v})
obtaining
m^{1},q^{1}
and
v^{1}
;
(v)
repeat the steps
(i)
–
(iv)
starting from
m=m^{1}
,
q=q^{1}
and
v=v^{1}
, thus obtaining
m^{2}
,
q^{2}
and
v^{2}
, and so forth.

The numerics arising from this procedure though turns out to be delicate for extreme values of the overlap, namely when
v-q
is really small, which in turn happens when
\lambda
is large (typically
>3
for Rademacher prior). The equation that seems to generate numerical instability is (
194
), and in particular the two contributions there appearing. With reference to the Rademacher prior, and the related Figure
4
, when
\lambda>3
the overlap gets close to
\sim 0.999
. At this value
1/(v-q)^{2}\sim 10^{6}
.
\tilde{q}
, that is also contributing to (
194
), on the contrary becomes really negative, and is such that
\hat{q}
is typically
\sim 10
near
\lambda\sim 3
. The subtraction of these two big numbers apparently dooms the iterations for larger SNRs. This was not the case in the Bayes-optimal setting, thanks to the simplifications introduced by the Nishimori identities. Indeed, from (
96
), (
97
), (
98
) and (
99
) we see that
1-m
appears at most at the first power in denominators. The only issue there was that
\tilde{V}
can grow exponentially fast, and this can be solved by allowing for a wide range of search of the solution of (
98
).

The fixed point of the MSE arising from (
183
)–(
190
) is compared with the fixed point (
141
), which corresponds to the MSE of the AMP proposed in
[
45
]
. The match between these two computations is excellent, as long as the SNR is not too large, because of the aforementioned numerical issues in iterating (
183
)–(
190
). The plot of Figure
4
is a compelling numerical confirmation of the arguments put forward in this section. The conclusion is the following: the AMP algorithm of
[
45
]
is solving a
replica symmetric approximation
to the TAP equations associated with the mismatched posterior distribution (
164
). This analysis, in turns, shows that despite the existing AMP algorithm
[
45
]
is aware of the noise structure/statistics, it turns out that it nevertheless makes an implicit assumption of i.i.d. Gaussian noise, and the noise structure is “only” exploited to enforce convergence despite this mismatched assumption, rather than as a source of improvement in statistical accuracy. In contrast, our AMP algorithm proposed in the next sections exploits noise structure for both convergence and statistical accuracy.

9
Towards an optimal AMP: AdaTAP formalism

We have previously shown that the AMP found in the literature for structured PCA
[
45
]
is sub-optimal. In this section we understand the fundamental reason behind this issue by generalizing the Adaptive Thouless-Anderson-Palmer (AdaTAP) formalism of
[
94
,
92
]
. Using our new insights we will then be able in the next section to cure the issue and derive a Bayes-optimal AMP. Like in the replica method and in particular Section
7.1
, a key ingredient will be to reduce the model to a quadratic one of the Ising type.

Let us mention that, to the best of our knowledge, it is the first time that AdaTAP is used for a planted model with a spike; usually the interaction matrix of the Ising-type model for which AdaTAP was designed is rotationally invariant. Our motivation is that in our setting, the spike being low-rank, this should not affect much the “macroscopic” properties of the data matrix compared to the null model, i.e., with pure noise, for which AdaTAP was developed. By macroscopic quantities we mean here density of eigenvalues, its cumulants, or the fact that the eigenbasis of the data remains “almost” uniformly (Haar) distributed in the set of orthogonal matrices in spite of the presence of the rank-one spike. Of course these are not proper arguments for its validity in the current setting. The real confirmation of the validity of AdaTAP for our setting will be the a-posteriori perfect match between the BAMP derived from AdaTAP (in particular its state evolution fixed point) and the replica prediction for the MMSE, obtained in a completely different manner.

9.1
The AdaTAP single-instance free entropy

Recall that the posterior distribution is given by (
21
). Denoting
{\mathbf{p}}:={\mathbf{x}}{\mathbf{x}}^{\intercal}/N
and
v:=\|{\mathbf{x}}\|^{2}/N
the trace of the matrix potential (
28
) can be expanded as follows:

\displaystyle{\rm Tr}V({\mathbf{Y}}-\lambda{\mathbf{p}})=C+\frac{\mu}{2}{\rm Tr}\big\{\lambda^{2}v^{2}-2\lambda{{\mathbf{Y}}{\mathbf{p}}}\big\}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\gamma}{4}{\rm Tr}\big\{\lambda^{4}v^{4}-4\lambda^{3}v^{2}{\mathbf{Y}}{\mathbf{p}}+4\lambda^{2}v{\mathbf{Y}}^{2}{\mathbf{p}}-4{\lambda}{\mathbf{Y}}^{3}{\mathbf{p}}+2\lambda^{2}{\mathbf{Y}}{\mathbf{p}}{\mathbf{Y}}{\mathbf{p}}\big\}

where
C
is independent of
{\mathbf{x}}
. Define the matrix polynomial:

\displaystyle{\mathbf{R}}(v,{\mathbf{Y}}):=-(\mu\lambda+\gamma\lambda^{3}v^{2}){\mathbf{Y}}+\gamma\lambda^{2}v{\mathbf{Y}}^{2}-\gamma\lambda{\mathbf{Y}}^{3}.

(199)

Then

\displaystyle-\frac{N}{2}{\rm Tr}V({\mathbf{Y}}-\lambda{\mathbf{p}})\propto

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{N}{4}\lambda^{2}v^{2}\Big(\mu+\frac{\gamma\lambda^{2}v^{2}}{2}\Big)-\frac{{\mathbf{x}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{R}}(v,{\mathbf{Y}}){\mathbf{x}}}{2}-\frac{N}{4}\gamma\lambda^{2}\Big(\frac{{\mathbf{x}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{Y}}{\mathbf{x}}}{N}\Big)^{2}.

(200)

The partition function of the model defined by (
27
) can then be written in the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}

\displaystyle\propto\int dP_{X}({\mathbf{x}})dvdf\delta(Nv-\|{\mathbf{x}}\|^{2})\delta(Nf-{\mathbf{x}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{Y}}{\mathbf{x}})

\displaystyle\qquad\times\exp\Big(-\frac{N}{4}\lambda^{2}v^{2}\Big(\mu+\frac{\gamma\lambda^{2}v^{2}}{2}\Big)-\frac{1}{2}{\mathbf{x}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{R}}(v,{\mathbf{Y}}){\mathbf{x}}-\frac{N}{4}{\gamma\lambda^{2}}f^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle=\int dvd\hat{v}dfd\hat{f}\exp\Big(N\hat{v}v+N\hat{f}f-\frac{N}{4}\lambda^{2}v^{2}\Big(\mu+\frac{\gamma\lambda^{2}v^{2}}{2}\Big)-\frac{N}{4}{\gamma\lambda^{2}}f^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\int dP_{X}({\mathbf{x}})\exp\Big(-\hat{v}\|{\mathbf{x}}\|^{2}-\hat{f}{\mathbf{x}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{Y}}{\mathbf{x}}-\frac{1}{2}{\mathbf{x}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{R}}(v,{\mathbf{Y}}){\mathbf{x}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\int dvd\hat{v}dfd\hat{f}\exp\Big(N\hat{v}v+N\hat{f}f-\frac{N}{4}\lambda^{2}v^{2}\Big(\mu+\frac{\gamma\lambda^{2}v^{2}}{2}\Big)-\frac{N}{4}{\gamma\lambda^{2}}f^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\int dP_{X}({\mathbf{x}})\exp\Big(\frac{1}{2}{{\mathbf{x}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{J}}(v,\hat{v},\hat{f},{\mathbf{Y}}){\mathbf{x}}}\Big),

(201)

where the overall symmetric interaction matrix of this “Ising model” is

\displaystyle{\mathbf{J}}(v,\hat{v},\hat{f},{\mathbf{Y}}):=-{\mathbf{R}}(v,{\mathbf{Y}})-2\hat{v}\boldsymbol{I}_{N}-2\hat{f}{\mathbf{Y}}.

(202)

Now, defining the free entropy at fixed
(v,\hat{v},\hat{f})

\displaystyle\Phi_{N}(v,\hat{v},\hat{f},{\mathbf{Y}}):=\ln\int dP_{X}({\mathbf{x}})\exp\Big(\frac{1}{2}{{\mathbf{x}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{J}}(v,\hat{v},\hat{f},{\mathbf{Y}}){\mathbf{x}}}\Big),

(203)

because the prior is factorized and we have an Ising-type of model, we can directly use the AdaTAP result
[
94
]
: it tells us that

\displaystyle\Phi_{N}(v,\hat{v},\hat{f},{\mathbf{Y}})=-{\rm extr}_{{\mathbf{m}},\boldsymbol{\tau},{\mathbf{V}}}\Big\{\frac{1}{2}{\mathbf{m}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{J}}(v,\hat{v},\hat{f},{\mathbf{Y}}){\mathbf{m}}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\ln\det\big(\boldsymbol{\Omega}-{\mathbf{J}}(v,\hat{v},\hat{f},{\mathbf{Y}})\big)-\frac{1}{2}{\mathbf{V}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{m}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\leq N}\ln(\tau_{i}-m_{i}^{2})

\displaystyle\quad-\sum_{i\leq N}\ln\int dP_{X}(x)\exp\Big(\frac{1}{2}V_{i}x^{2}+\big(({\mathbf{J}}(v,\hat{v},\hat{f},{\mathbf{Y}}){\mathbf{m}})_{i}-V_{i}m_{i}\big)x\Big)\Big\}+o_{N}(1).

(204)

The extremization is over
({\mathbf{m}},\boldsymbol{\tau},{\mathbf{V}})\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\times(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{N})^{2}
,
{\mathbf{m}}^{2}=(m_{i}^{2})_{i\leq N}
, and the diagonal matrix

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Omega}:={\rm diag}({\mathbf{V}}+({\boldsymbol{\tau}}-{\mathbf{m}}^{2})^{-1}).

(205)

Let the bracket notation
\langle\,\cdot\,\rangle
be used as expectation with respect to the posterior (
21
), while
\langle\,\cdot\,\rangle_{\backslash i}
is the mean with respect to the Gibbs measure of the “cavity graph” where
(J_{ij})_{j}
are set to
0
. Define also the cavity fields

h_{i}:=({\mathbf{J}}{\mathbf{x}})_{i}.

The various variables at their extremum values are (asymptotically exact approximations to) the marginals means, second moments and variances of the cavity fields

m_{i}=\langle x_{i}\rangle,\quad\tau_{i}=\langle x_{i}^{2}\rangle,\quad V_{i}=\langle h_{i}^{2}\rangle_{\backslash i}-\langle h_{i}\rangle_{\backslash i}^{2}.

From the AdaTAP free entropy at fixed
(v,\hat{v},\hat{f})
we can compute the total log-partition function by saddle-point and get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\ln\mathcal{Z}({\mathbf{Y}})\propto o_{N}(1)

\displaystyle\quad+{\rm extr}\Big\{\hat{v}v+\hat{f}f-\frac{1}{4}\lambda^{2}v^{2}\Big(\mu+\frac{\gamma\lambda^{2}v^{2}}{2}\Big)-\frac{1}{4}{\gamma\lambda^{2}}f^{2}+\Phi_{N}(v,\hat{v},\hat{f},{\mathbf{Y}})\Big\}

(206)

where the extremization is over
(v,\hat{v},f,\hat{f})
.

9.2
Saddle point: reduction to an Ising model, AdaTAP equations and optimal pre-processing of the data

By extremization of the AdaTAP single-instance free entropy (
9.1
) we derive the AdaTAP equations. We start with the intensive parameters. The extremization with respect to
f
is trivial and gives

\hat{f}=\frac{1}{2}\gamma\lambda^{2}f.

So the leading order of the AdaTAP free entropy simplifies to

\displaystyle{\rm extr}_{v,\hat{v},f}\Big\{\hat{v}v+\frac{1}{4}\gamma\lambda^{2}f^{2}-\frac{1}{4}\lambda^{2}v^{2}\Big(\mu+\frac{\gamma\lambda^{2}v^{2}}{2}\Big)+\Phi_{N}\Big(v,\hat{v},\frac{1}{2}\gamma\lambda^{2}f,{\mathbf{Y}}\Big)\Big\}.

(207)

The remaining saddle point equations can simply be written down. But this is not necessary as
the solution of the three remaining intensive order parameters at the saddle point is simply deduced from their physical meaning, concentration properties, and the Nishimori identity: in the large size limit,

v\to\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}\langle\|{\mathbf{x}}\|^{2}\rangle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}\|{\mathbf{X}}^{*}\|^{2}=1,

as well as (recall (
87
))

f\to\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}\langle{\mathbf{x}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{Y}}{\mathbf{x}}\rangle=\lambda.

Moreover we know that

\hat{v}\to 0

because the prior is already enforcing the constraint that
v=\|{\mathbf{x}}\|^{2}/N\to 1
in (
9.1
) without the need to introducing a further, redundant, delta constraint; note that for Rademacher or spherical prior this is simply true as no delta function is needed. Therefore the AdaTAP free entropy becomes

\displaystyle\frac{1}{8}\gamma\lambda^{4}-\frac{1}{4}\mu\lambda^{2}+\Phi_{N}\Big(1,0,\frac{1}{2}\gamma\lambda^{3},{\mathbf{Y}}\Big)+o_{N}(1).

(208)

From this AdaTAP free entropy we see that the values of the marginal means and variances correspond to the solution of the variational problem (
204
) with interaction matrix

\displaystyle{\mathbf{J}}\Big(1,0,\frac{1}{2}\gamma\lambda^{3},{\mathbf{Y}}\Big)=\mu\lambda{\mathbf{Y}}-\gamma\lambda^{2}{\mathbf{Y}}^{2}+\gamma\lambda{\mathbf{Y}}^{3}=:{\mathbf{J}}({\mathbf{Y}}).

(209)

So we end-up with the following effective partition function of an Ising-like model:

\displaystyle\int dP_{X}({\mathbf{x}})\exp\Big(\frac{1}{2}{{\mathbf{x}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{J}}({\mathbf{Y}}){\mathbf{x}}}\Big).

(210)

This shows that the original model is equivalent to an Ising model with interaction matrix
{\mathbf{J}}({\mathbf{Y}})
, which can thus be interpreted as a
Bayes-optimal pre-processing of the data
. This will be verified in Section
10
, as the use of
{\mathbf{J}}({\mathbf{Y}})
instead of
{\mathbf{Y}}
will turn AMP into an optimal algorithm. Ising models like this are precisely studied in
[
94
]
and we can therefore again exploit directly the AdaTAP formalism.
Let

\displaystyle\eta_{i}({\mathbf{J}},{\mathbf{m}},V_{i})

\displaystyle:=\frac{\int dP_{X}(x)\,x\,e^{\frac{1}{2}V_{i}x^{2}+(({\mathbf{J}}{\mathbf{m}})_{i}-V_{i}m_{i})x}}{\int dP_{X}(x)e^{\frac{1}{2}V_{i}x^{2}+(({\mathbf{J}}{\mathbf{m}})_{i}-V_{i}m_{i})x}},

(211)

\displaystyle g_{i}({\mathbf{J}},{\mathbf{m}},V_{i})

\displaystyle:=\frac{\int dP_{X}(x)\,x^{2}\,e^{\frac{1}{2}V_{i}x^{2}+(({\mathbf{J}}{\mathbf{m}})_{i}-V_{i}m_{i})x}}{\int dP_{X}(x)e^{\frac{1}{2}V_{i}x^{2}+(({\mathbf{J}}{\mathbf{m}})_{i}-V_{i}m_{i})x}}.

(212)

The associated AdaTAP equations over

({\mathbf{m}},{\boldsymbol{\tau}},{\mathbf{V}})
, namely the saddle point equations associated with the AdaTAP free entropy (
204
) with
{\mathbf{J}}(v,\hat{v},\hat{f},{\mathbf{Y}})
replaced by
{\mathbf{J}}={\mathbf{J}}({\mathbf{Y}})
, read

\displaystyle m_{i}

\displaystyle=\eta_{i}({\mathbf{J}},{\mathbf{m}},V_{i}),

(213)

\displaystyle\tau_{i}

\displaystyle=g_{i}({\mathbf{J}},{\mathbf{m}},V_{i}),

(214)

\displaystyle\tau_{i}-m_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle=\big([{\rm diag}({\mathbf{V}}+({\boldsymbol{\tau}}-{\mathbf{m}}^{2})^{-1})-{\mathbf{J}}]^{-1}\big)_{ii},

(215)

where the last equation is understood as an implicit equation for
{\mathbf{V}}
.

9.3
Optimal pre-processing for the order 6 potential

Let us now consider the pure sestic ensemble with matrix potential
V(x)=\xi x^{6}/6
, and
\xi=27/80
.
With the same notations as in the previous section, the trace of the matrix potential now reads

\displaystyle\frac{6}{\xi}{\rm Tr}V(\mathbf{Y}-\lambda\mathbf{p})=C+{\rm Tr}\mathbf{p}\Big[-6\lambda\mathbf{Y}^{5}+6\lambda^{2}v{\mathbf{Y}}^{4}-6\lambda^{3}v^{2}{\mathbf{Y}}^{3}+6\lambda^{4}v^{3}{\mathbf{Y}}^{2}-6\lambda^{5}v^{4}{\mathbf{Y}}\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad+6\lambda^{2}f_{3}f_{1}+3\lambda^{2}f_{2}^{2}-12\lambda^{3}vf_{2}f_{1}-2\lambda^{3}f_{1}^{3}+9\lambda^{4}v^{2}f_{1}^{2}+\lambda^{6}v^{6}

(216)

where we have introduced the parameters
f_{j}={\rm Tr}{\mathbf{Y}}^{j}\mathbf{p}
,
j=1,2,3
. Hence the Hamiltonian in the posterior measure (
21
) can be written as

\displaystyle-\frac{N}{2}{\rm Tr}V({\mathbf{Y}}-\lambda\mathbf{p})\propto-\frac{\xi\mathbf{x}^{\intercal}\mathbf{R}_{6}(v,{\mathbf{Y}})\mathbf{x}}{2}

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{N\xi}{2}\Big(\lambda^{2}f_{3}f_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{2}f_{2}^{2}-2\lambda^{3}vf_{2}f_{1}-\frac{1}{3}\lambda^{3}f_{1}^{3}+\frac{3}{2}\lambda^{4}v^{2}f_{1}^{2}+\frac{\lambda^{6}v^{6}}{6}\Big)

(217)

with
\mathbf{R}_{6}(v,{\mathbf{Y}})=-\lambda\mathbf{Y}^{5}+\lambda^{2}v{\mathbf{Y}}^{4}-\lambda^{3}v^{2}{\mathbf{Y}}^{3}+\lambda^{4}v^{3}{\mathbf{Y}}^{2}-\lambda^{5}v^{4}{\mathbf{Y}}
.

Now, as for the quartic potential, we need to fix the order parameters
(f_{j})_{j=1,2,3}
and
v
introducing the Fourier conjugates
(\hat{f}_{j})_{j=1,2,3}
and
\hat{v}
, which produces additional two-body interaction terms. The partition function then reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}=\int dvd\hat{v}\prod_{j=1}^{3}df_{j}d\hat{f}_{j}\exp\Big[N\hat{v}v+N\sum_{j=1}^{3}\hat{f}_{j}f_{j}-\frac{N\xi}{2}\Big(\lambda^{2}f_{3}f_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{2}f_{2}^{2}-2\lambda^{3}vf_{2}f_{1}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{3}\lambda^{3}f_{1}^{3}+\frac{3}{2}\lambda^{4}v^{2}f_{1}^{2}+\frac{\lambda^{6}v^{6}}{6}\Big)\Big]\int dP_{X}({\mathbf{x}})\exp\Big(\frac{1}{2}{{\mathbf{x}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{J}}(v,\hat{v},(\hat{f}_{j})_{j=1,2,3},{\mathbf{Y}}){\mathbf{x}}}\Big)\,,

(218)

where

{\mathbf{J}}(v,\hat{v},(\hat{f}_{j})_{j=1,2,3},{\mathbf{Y}})=\xi\lambda{\mathbf{Y}}^{5}-\xi\lambda^{2}v{\mathbf{Y}}^{4}+(\xi\lambda^{3}v^{2}-2\hat{f}_{3}){\mathbf{Y}}^{3}-(\xi\lambda^{4}v^{3}+2\hat{f}_{2}){\mathbf{Y}}^{2}\\
+(\xi\lambda^{5}v^{4}-2\hat{f}_{1}){\mathbf{Y}}-2\hat{v}\boldsymbol{I}_{N}\,.

(219)

Using the Nishimori identities we are already able to fix the values of
v
and
\hat{v}
to
1
and
0
respectively.
Furthermore, in order to have an explicit
{\mathbf{J}}({\mathbf{Y}})
, we also need to fix the remaining
\hat{f}_{j}
’s. Without repeating all the procedure, we notice that their values are determined by the argument of the first exponential in (
9.3
). In particular, it suffices to equate to zero its gradient w.r.t.
(f_{j})_{j=1,2,3}
to obtain the system of equations:

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\xi\lambda^{2}}{2}f_{3}-\xi\lambda^{3}f_{2}-\frac{\xi\lambda^{3}}{2}f_{1}^{2}+\frac{3\xi\lambda^{4}}{2}f_{1}\,,

(220)

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\xi\lambda^{2}}{2}f_{2}-\xi\lambda^{3}f_{1}\,,

(221)

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{\xi\lambda^{2}}{2}f_{1}\,,

(222)

where we have already set
v=1\,,\,\,\hat{v}=0
.
The values of
(f_{j})_{j=1,2,3}
can be fixed again by the Nishimori identities; indeed, in the thermodynamic limit one has

\displaystyle f_{1}\to\lambda\,,\quad f_{2}\to\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}\langle\mathbf{x}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{Y}}^{2}{\mathbf{x}}\rangle=1+\lambda^{2}

(223)

and also

\displaystyle f_{3}\to\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}\langle\mathbf{x}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{Y}}^{3}{\mathbf{x}}\rangle=\lambda^{3}+2\lambda+\lambda\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{1}{N}{\rm Tr}{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{X}}^{*}{\mathbf{X}}^{*\intercal}\Big]^{2}=\lambda^{3}+2\lambda\,.

(224)

The expectation of
({\rm Tr}{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{X}}^{*}{\mathbf{X}}^{*\intercal}/N)^{2}
vanishes because the variable
{\rm Tr}{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{X}}^{*}{\mathbf{X}}^{*\intercal}/N
concentrates around
0
. Gathering all these results and plugging them into
{\mathbf{J}}
we finally get the pre-processing matrix that should lead to an AMP algorithm with Bayes-optimal performance:

\displaystyle{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{Y})

\displaystyle={\mathbf{J}}\Big(1,0,\hat{f}_{1}=\xi\frac{\lambda^{5}}{2},\hat{f}_{2}=\xi\frac{\lambda^{2}-\lambda^{4}}{2},\hat{f}_{3}=\xi\frac{\lambda^{3}}{2},{\mathbf{Y}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\xi\lambda{\mathbf{Y}}^{5}-\xi\lambda^{2}{\mathbf{Y}}^{4}-\xi\lambda^{2}{\mathbf{Y}}^{2}\,.

(225)

9.4
Simplifying the AdaTAP equations by self-averaging of the Onsager reaction term

The variances
V_{i}
are expected to be self-averaging with respect to the interaction matrix, i.e., in the large size limit
V_{i}=\bar{V}:=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{J}}V_{i}
. The computation we are going to carry out now could be performed in various ways leading to different but equivalent expressions. For pedagogical reasons we take a path that remains as close as possible to the approach of
[
94
]
. Following this reference we compute the expectation of the AdaTAP equation for
{\mathbf{V}}
. In this section, all quantities
{\mathbf{V}}
,
{\mathbf{m}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
are fixed to a solution of the AdaTAP equations (
213
)–(
215
).

We start from the convenient identity

\displaystyle\big([\boldsymbol{\Omega}-{\mathbf{J}}]^{-1}\big)_{ii}=\partial_{\Omega_{ii}}\ln\det(\boldsymbol{\Omega}-{\mathbf{J}}).

(226)

We are going to average the right-hand side. As for a Gaussian model there is no spin glass phase and strong concentrations take place, the quenched and annealed averages match
[
94
]
: we can thus simply compute the logarithm of the average of the determinant. A Gaussian identity then gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\det(\boldsymbol{\Omega}-{\mathbf{J}})^{-1/2}=\int\frac{d{\mathbf{z}}}{(2\pi)^{N/2}}\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}{\mathbf{z}}^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{\Omega}{\mathbf{z}}\Big)\mathbb{E}\exp\Big(\frac{1}{2}{\mathbf{z}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{J}}{\mathbf{z}}\Big).

(227)

We denote
{\mathbf{J}}=\sum_{k\leq 3}c_{k}{\mathbf{Y}}^{k}
where
{\mathbf{c}}=(\mu\lambda,-\gamma\lambda^{2},\gamma\lambda)
. The term we need to compute therefore reads

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\exp\Big(\frac{1}{2}{\mathbf{z}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{J}}{\mathbf{z}}\Big)=\mathbb{E}\exp\frac{1}{2}({\mathbf{z}}^{\intercal}(c_{1}{\mathbf{Y}}+c_{2}{\mathbf{Y}}^{2}+c_{3}{\mathbf{Y}}^{3}){\mathbf{z}})

(228)

Define the order parameters

\displaystyle p:=\frac{1}{N}{\mathbf{z}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{X}}^{*},\quad v:=\frac{1}{N}\|{\mathbf{z}}\|^{2},\quad p_{D}:=\frac{1}{N}({\mathbf{O}}{\mathbf{z}})^{\intercal}{\mathbf{D}}{\mathbf{O}}{\mathbf{X}}^{*}.

(229)

We also have
\|{\mathbf{X}}^{*}\|^{2}/N=1+o_{N}(1)
.
Our goal is to identify the generalized spherical integral (
41
). Replacing
{\mathbf{Y}}
by
\lambda{\mathbf{p}}^{*}+{\mathbf{O}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{D}}{\mathbf{O}}
(with
{\mathbf{p}}^{*}:={\mathbf{X}}^{*}{\mathbf{X}}^{*\intercal}/N
) we expand the various terms. The first term is then simply

\displaystyle c_{1}{\mathbf{z}}^{\intercal}\big(\lambda{\mathbf{p}}^{*}+{\mathbf{O}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{D}}{\mathbf{O}}\big){\mathbf{z}}=c_{1}\big(\lambda Np^{2}+({\mathbf{O}}{\mathbf{z}})^{\intercal}{\mathbf{D}}{\mathbf{O}}{\mathbf{z}}\big).

(230)

The second term is

\displaystyle c_{2}{\mathbf{z}}^{\intercal}\big(\lambda^{2}(\|{\mathbf{X}}^{*}\|^{2}/N){\mathbf{p}}^{*}+\lambda{\mathbf{p}}^{*}{\mathbf{O}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{D}}{\mathbf{O}}+\lambda{\mathbf{O}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{D}}{\mathbf{O}}{\mathbf{p}}^{*}+{\mathbf{O}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{D}}^{2}{\mathbf{O}}\big){\mathbf{z}}

\displaystyle=c_{2}\big(N\lambda^{2}p^{2}+2N\lambda pp_{D}+({\mathbf{O}}{\mathbf{z}})^{\intercal}{\mathbf{D}}^{2}{\mathbf{O}}{\mathbf{z}}\big)+o(N).

(231)

Finally the last term is a bit more cumbersome:

\displaystyle c_{3}{\mathbf{z}}^{\intercal}\big(\lambda^{3}(\|{\mathbf{X}}^{*}\|^{4}/N^{2}){\mathbf{p}}^{*}+\lambda^{2}{\mathbf{p}}^{*}{\mathbf{O}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{D}}{\mathbf{O}}{\mathbf{p}}^{*}+\lambda^{2}(\|{\mathbf{X}}^{*}\|^{2}/N){\mathbf{O}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{D}}{\mathbf{O}}{\mathbf{p}}^{*}+\lambda{\mathbf{O}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{D}}^{2}{\mathbf{O}}{\mathbf{p}}^{*}

\displaystyle\quad+\lambda^{2}(\|{\mathbf{X}}^{*}\|^{2}/N){\mathbf{p}}^{*}{\mathbf{O}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{D}}{\mathbf{O}}+\lambda{\mathbf{p}}^{*}{\mathbf{O}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{D}}^{2}{\mathbf{O}}+\lambda{\mathbf{O}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{D}}{\mathbf{O}}{\mathbf{p}}^{*}{\mathbf{O}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{D}}{\mathbf{O}}+{\mathbf{O}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{D}}^{3}{\mathbf{O}}\big){\mathbf{z}}

\displaystyle=c_{3}\big(N\lambda^{3}p^{2}+\lambda^{2}p^{2}({\mathbf{O}}{\mathbf{X}}^{*})^{\intercal}{\mathbf{D}}{\mathbf{O}}{\mathbf{X}}^{*}+2\lambda p({\mathbf{O}}{\mathbf{z}})^{\intercal}{\mathbf{D}}^{2}{\mathbf{O}}{\mathbf{X}}^{*}

\displaystyle\quad+2N\lambda^{2}pp_{D}+\lambda Np_{D}^{2}+({\mathbf{O}}{\mathbf{z}})^{\intercal}{\mathbf{D}}^{3}{\mathbf{O}}{\mathbf{z}}\big)+o(N).

(232)

Combining all we reach

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\exp\Big(\frac{1}{2}{\mathbf{z}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{J}}{\mathbf{z}}\Big)=\int d{\boldsymbol{\tau}}d\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\,\exp\Big(NK+\frac{1}{2}\hat{v}\|{\mathbf{z}}\|^{2}-\frac{N}{2}\hat{v}v+o(N)\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{O}}\exp\Big(({\mathbf{O}}{\mathbf{z}})^{\intercal}{\mathbf{C}}_{{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbf{z}}}{\mathbf{O}}{\mathbf{z}}+({\mathbf{O}}{\mathbf{X}}^{*})^{\intercal}{\mathbf{C}}_{*,*}{\mathbf{O}}{\mathbf{X}}^{*}+({\mathbf{O}}{\mathbf{X}}^{*})^{\intercal}{\mathbf{C}}_{{\mathbf{z}},*}{\mathbf{O}}{\mathbf{z}}\Big)

(233)

with
d{\boldsymbol{\tau}}:=(dp,dv,dp_{D})
and
d\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}}:=(d\hat{p},d\hat{v},d\hat{p}_{D})
, and (all coupling matrices below are
N\times N
and symmetric)

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\big(\mu\lambda^{2}p^{2}-\gamma\lambda^{2}(\lambda^{2}p^{2}+2\lambda pp_{D})+\gamma\lambda(\lambda^{3}p^{2}+2\lambda^{2}pp_{D}+\lambda p_{D}^{2})+\hat{p}p+\hat{p}_{D}p_{D}\big),

\displaystyle{\mathbf{C}}_{*,*}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\gamma\lambda^{3}p^{2}{\mathbf{D}},

\displaystyle{\mathbf{C}}_{{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbf{z}}}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\big(\mu\lambda{\mathbf{D}}-\gamma\lambda^{2}{\mathbf{D}}^{2}+\gamma\lambda{\mathbf{D}}^{3}\big),

\displaystyle{\mathbf{C}}_{{\mathbf{z}},*}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\big(-\hat{p}I_{N}-\hat{p}_{D}{\mathbf{D}}+2\gamma\lambda^{2}p{\mathbf{D}}^{2}\big).

Note the asymmetry for the variable
\hat{v}
compared to the other hat-variables, which has not been injected in the definition of the coupling matrices as the others, but instead leads to a term appearing explicitly in (
233
) (both choices are equivalently valid ones). The term averaged over
{\mathbf{O}}
is an inhomogeneous spherical integral as studied in Section
6
. In particular, we are in the case of Section
6.2.3
with
\ell\in\{0,1\}
with the exception that
{\mathbf{X}}^{*}
also (playing the role of the
0
th replica) has a non-zero self-coupling. So this trivial modification of the computation of Section
6.2.3
yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}e^{\frac{1}{2}{\mathbf{z}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{J}}{\mathbf{z}}}

\displaystyle=\int d{\boldsymbol{\tau}}d\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\,\exp\Big(NK+\frac{1}{2}\hat{v}\|{\mathbf{z}}\|^{2}-\frac{N}{2}\hat{v}v+NI_{\mathbf{C}}(p,v,\hat{p},\hat{p}_{D})+o(N)\Big)

where the
2\times 2
random coupling matrix
{\mathbf{C}}
has entries

\displaystyle 2C_{00}

\displaystyle=\gamma\lambda^{3}p^{2}D,

(234)

\displaystyle 2C_{11}

\displaystyle=\mu\lambda D-\gamma\lambda^{2}D^{2}+\gamma\lambda D^{3},

(235)

\displaystyle 2C_{01}=2C_{10}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(-\hat{p}-\hat{p}_{D}D+2\gamma\lambda^{2}pD^{2}),

(236)

with
D\sim\rho
drawn from the noise asymptotic spectral density, and

\displaystyle I_{\mathbf{C}}(p,v,\hat{p},\hat{p}_{D})=\frac{1}{2}{\rm extr}_{(\tilde{v}_{0},\tilde{v},\tilde{p})}\Big\{\tilde{v}_{0}+2\tilde{p}p+\tilde{v}v

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathbb{E}\ln\big((\tilde{v}_{0}-2C_{00})(\tilde{v}-2C_{11})-(\tilde{p}-2C_{01})^{2}\big)\Big\}-\frac{1}{2}\ln(v-p^{2})-1.

(237)

One can check that
I_{\mathbf{C}}
is null when
C_{00}=C_{11}=C_{01}
as it should. Therefore equation (
227
) becomes at leading exponential order

\displaystyle\ln\mathbb{E}\det(\boldsymbol{\Omega}-{\mathbf{J}})^{-1/2}

\displaystyle\qquad=\ln\int\frac{d{\mathbf{z}}}{(2\pi)^{N/2}}d{\boldsymbol{\tau}}d\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}{\mathbf{z}}^{\intercal}(\boldsymbol{\Omega}-\hat{v}I_{N}){\mathbf{z}}+NK-\frac{N}{2}\hat{v}v+NI_{\mathbf{C}}+o(N)\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad=\ln\int d{\boldsymbol{\tau}}d\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\exp\Big(NK-\frac{N}{2}\hat{v}v+NI_{\mathbf{C}}-\frac{1}{2}\ln\det(\boldsymbol{\Omega}-\hat{v}I_{N})+o(N)\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad={\rm extr}\Big\{NK-\frac{N}{2}\hat{v}v+NI_{\mathbf{C}}-\frac{1}{2}\ln\det(\boldsymbol{\Omega}-\hat{v}I_{N})\Big\}+o(N),

where we used Gaussian integration followed by a saddle point estimation. By the aforementioned strong concentration properties of the Gaussian model, this is also equal to
-\frac{1}{2}\ln\mathbb{E}\det(\boldsymbol{\Omega}-{\mathbf{J}})\approx-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\ln\det(\boldsymbol{\Omega}-{\mathbf{J}})
so we reach at leading order

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\ln\det(\boldsymbol{\Omega}-{\mathbf{J}})

\displaystyle\approx{\rm extr}\big\{-2NK+N\hat{v}v-2NI_{\mathbf{C}}+\ln\det(\boldsymbol{\Omega}-\hat{v}I_{N})\big\}

\displaystyle={\rm extr}_{(\hat{v},v)}\Big\{N\hat{v}v+\sum_{i\leq N}\ln(\Omega_{ii}-\hat{v})-2N\tilde{G}(v)\Big\}

(238)

where the extremization is over all variables and

\displaystyle\tilde{G}(v):={\rm extr}_{(p,p_{D},\hat{p},\hat{p}_{D})}\big\{I_{\mathbf{C}}(p,v,\hat{p},\hat{p}_{D})+K(p,p_{D},\hat{p},\hat{p}_{D})\big\}.

(239)

This is the analogue of the G-function appearing, e.g., in
[
94
]
. The extremization over
\hat{v}
in (
238
) yields that at the saddle point,

v=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\leq N}\frac{1}{\Omega_{ii}-\hat{v}}.

Moreover, combining the TAP equation (
215
) with (
226
) and (
238
) we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\tau_{i}-m_{i}^{2})=\partial_{\Omega_{ii}}\mathbb{E}\ln\det(\boldsymbol{\Omega}-{\mathbf{J}})=\frac{1}{\Omega_{ii}-\hat{v}}

(240)

where
\hat{v}
is evaluated at its saddle point value. Therefore, summing over
i
the last identity and recalling the definition of
\Omega_{ii}
we reach

\displaystyle\bar{\chi}:=\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}\sum_{i\leq N}(\tau_{i}-m_{i}^{2})=v=\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}\sum_{i\leq N}\frac{1}{V_{i}+(\tau_{i}-m_{i}^{2})^{-1}-\hat{v}}.

(241)

Under the concentration assumption
V_{i}=\bar{V}
for all
i\leq N
, this identity implies

\displaystyle V_{i}=\hat{v}.

(242)

Additionally the saddle point equation for
v
extracted from (
238
) yields

\displaystyle\hat{v}=2\partial_{v}\tilde{G}(v)|_{v=\bar{\chi}}\quad\Rightarrow\quad V_{i}=\bar{V}:=2\partial_{v}\tilde{G}(v)|_{v=\bar{\chi}}.

(243)

The variable
\bar{\chi}
is instance-independent and can be deduced from our replica theory: it is equal to twice the MMSE (
100
), namely,

\displaystyle\bar{\chi}=1-m^{2}

(244)

where
m
is solution to the replica fixed point equations (
94
)–(
99
). Computing
\bar{V}
from (
243
) is then easy, as taking a derivative w.r.t.
v
of
\tilde{G}(v)
is straightforward: all the quantities appearing on the right-hand side of (
239
) are at the saddle point, so it simply amounts to a partial derivative of (
237
). It gives

\displaystyle\bar{V}=\tilde{v}-\frac{1}{1-m^{2}-p^{2}}

(245)

where
\tilde{v}=\tilde{v}(p,v)
takes its saddle point value from (
237
) while
p=p(v)
from (
239
) with
v=\bar{\chi}
fixed.

Thanks to these simplifications the AdaTAP equation reads in the large size limit

\displaystyle m_{i}

\displaystyle=\eta_{i}({\mathbf{J}},{\mathbf{m}},\bar{V}).

(246)

Or, when written in a fashion closer to the form of AMP algorithms, the AdaTAP equations read

\displaystyle{\mathbf{f}}={\mathbf{J}}{\mathbf{m}}-\bar{V}{\mathbf{m}},\qquad{\mathbf{m}}=\eta_{\bar{V}}({\mathbf{f}}),

(247)

where the “denoiser”, which is applied component-wise above, is

\displaystyle\eta_{\bar{V}}(f):=\frac{\int dP_{X}(x)\,x\,e^{\frac{1}{2}\bar{V}x^{2}+fx}}{\int dP_{X}(x)e^{\frac{1}{2}\bar{V}x^{2}+fx}}.

(248)

10
Approximate message passing, optimally

We will now describe an AMP algorithm that matches the replica prediction for the minimum mean-square error. We therefore conjecture it to be Bayes-optimal and refer to it as BAMP. The main difference between this new AMP and the previously proposed one for structured PCA is that it is constructed from iterates based on the pre-processed matrix
{\mathbf{J}}({\mathbf{Y}})
rather than
{\mathbf{Y}}
as in
[
45
]
. Consequently, the Onsager reaction terms will have to be adapted. Finally, inspired by the structure of BAMP, we present a choice of denoisers in the AMP of
[
45
]
which alternates between linear functions and posterior means given all the previous iterates (AMP with Alternating Posteriors, AMP-AP). The numerical results of the following section will show that AMP-AP matches the BAMP performance and, therefore, the replica prediction.

10.1
BAMP: Bayes-optimal AMP

The AdaTAP approach described in Section
9
suggests that, in order to achieve Bayes-optimal performance, one should consider the BAMP iteration which is of the form

{\mathbf{f}}^{t}={\mathbf{J}}({\mathbf{Y}}){\mathbf{u}}^{t}-\sum_{i=1}^{t}{\sf c}_{t,i}{\mathbf{u}}^{i},\quad{\mathbf{u}}^{t+1}=g_{t+1}({\mathbf{f}}^{t}),\quad t\geq 1.

(249)

As in the AMP iteration (
138
), the
denoiser
function
g_{t+1}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is continuously differentiable, Lipschitz and applied component-wise. Crucially, the Onsager coefficients
\{{\sf c}_{t,i}\}_{i\in[t],t\geq 1}
need to ensure that, conditioned on the signal, the empirical distribution of the iterate
{\mathbf{f}}^{t}
is Gaussian, namely, the convergence result in (
139
) holds for some mean vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t}
and covariance matrix
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{t}
.

We highlight that the matrix
{\mathbf{Y}}
in (
138
) is replaced by the matrix
{\mathbf{J}}({\mathbf{Y}})
in (
249
). This means that the state evolution result of
[
45
]
cannot be applied and the Onsager coefficients
\{{\sf c}_{t,i}\}_{i\in[t],t\geq 1}
will have a different form with respect to
\{{\sf b}_{t,i}\}_{i\in[t],t\geq 1}
.

In what follows, we will consider the general case in which
{\mathbf{J}}({\mathbf{Y}})
is an arbitrary polynomial of degree
K
in
{\mathbf{Y}}
, namely,

{\mathbf{J}}({\mathbf{Y}})=\sum_{i\leq K}c_{i}{\mathbf{Y}}^{i}.

To compute
\{{\sf c}_{t,i}\}_{i\in[t],t\geq 1}
and obtain a state evolution result for the iteration (
249
), the key idea is to map the first
T
iterations of (
249
) to the first
K\times T
iterations of an
auxiliary
AMP with iterates
(\tilde{\mathbf{z}}^{t},\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{t})_{t\in[KT]}
and denoisers
\{\tilde{h}_{t+1}\}_{t\in[KT]}
, whose state evolution can be deduced from
[
45
]
. The denoisers
\{\tilde{h}_{t+1}\}_{t\in[KT]}
of this auxiliary AMP are chosen so that, for
t\in[T]
and
\ell\in[K]
,

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{K(t-1)+\ell}-{\mathbf{Y}}^{\ell-1}{\mathbf{u}}^{t}\|_{2}^{2}=0.

(250)

More specifically, for
t\in[T]
and
\ell\in\{2,\ldots,K\}
, the denoiser
\tilde{h}_{K(t-1)+\ell}
giving
\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{K(t-1)+\ell}
is a linear combinations of the past iterates
\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{1},\ldots,\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{K(t-1)+\ell-1}
and of
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}^{K(t-1)+\ell-1}
; furthermore, the coefficients of these linear combinations are chosen to ensure that
\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{K(t-1)+\ell}\approx{\mathbf{Y}}^{\ell-1}{\mathbf{u}}^{t}
. Hence, from
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}^{Kt}
and
(\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{K(t-1)+\ell})_{\ell\in\{2,\ldots,K\}}
, one obtains
({\mathbf{Y}}^{\ell}{\mathbf{u}}^{t})_{\ell\in[K]}
(up to an
o_{N}(1)
error). As a result,
{\mathbf{J}}({\mathbf{Y}}){\mathbf{u}}^{t}
can be expressed as a linear combination of
(\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{1},\ldots,\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{Kt},\tilde{\mathbf{z}}^{Kt})
, which in turn is a linear combination of
(i)
the past iterates
\{{\mathbf{u}}^{i}\}_{i\in[t]}
,
(ii)

the signal
{\mathbf{X}}^{*}
, plus
(iii)
independent Gaussian noise. By inspecting the coefficients of this linear combination, one deduces
(i)
the values of
the Onsager coefficients
\{{\sf c}_{t,i}\}_{i\in[t],t\geq 1}
(as the coefficients multiplying the past iterates
\{{\mathbf{u}}^{i}\}_{i\in[t]}
),
(ii)
the mean
\mu_{t}
(as the coefficient multiplying the signal
{\mathbf{X}}^{*}
), and
(iii)
the covariance matrix
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{t}
(as the covariance matrix of the remaining noise terms). Finally, by making
\tilde{h}_{Kt+1}
depend on
g_{t+1}
, we enforce that
\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{Kt+1}\approx{\mathbf{u}}^{t+1}
. We highlight that the auxiliary AMP is employed purely as a proof technique. Its formal description is deferred to Appendix
C.1
, and its state evolution follows in Appendix
C.2
.

For simplicity, we assume to have access to an initialization
{\mathbf{u}}^{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
, which is independent of the noise
{\mathbf{Z}}
and has a strictly positive correlation with
{\mathbf{X}}^{*}
, i.e.,

({\mathbf{X}}^{*},{\mathbf{u}}^{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{W_{2}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}(X^{*},U_{1}),\quad\mathbb{E}[X^{*}\,U_{1}]:=\epsilon>0,\quad\mathbb{E}[U_{1}^{2}]=1.

(251)

The requirement (
251
) is rather standard in the analysis of AMP algorithms. However, as having access to such an initialization is often impractical, a recent line of work has designed AMP iterations which are initialized with the eigenvector of the data matrix
{\mathbf{Y}}
associated to the largest eigenvalue, see
[
86
,
84
,
110
]
. By following the approach detailed in
[
84
]
, one can design a Bayes-optimal AMP with spectral initialization. As this would be out of the scope of the current contribution – whose goal is to obtain an algorithm with a Bayes-optimal
fixed point
– we will not pursue this extension here.

10.2
Onsager coefficients and state evolution recursion

We now detail the calculation of the Onsager coefficients
\{{\sf c}_{t,i}\}_{i\in[t],t\geq 1}
and of the state evolution parameters
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{t}
associated to the AMP algorithm (
249
). We obtain these quantities from the state evolution recursion of the auxiliary AMP which, up to a
o_{N}(1)
error, tracks
({\mathbf{Y}}^{\ell-1}{\mathbf{u}}^{t})_{\ell\in[K]}
and, as such, has a number of iterations
K
times larger. To express the latter, we define a number of auxiliary quantities: the vector
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{Kt}\in\mathbb{R}^{Kt}
, the matrices
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{Kt},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{Kt},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{Kt},\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{Kt}\in\mathbb{R}^{Kt\times Kt}
, and the coefficients
\{\alpha_{i,j}\}_{j\in[i],i\in[Kt]}
,
\{\beta_{i,j}\}_{j\in[\lfloor(i-1)/K\rfloor+1],i\in[Kt]}
,
\{\gamma_{i}\}_{i\in[Kt]}
,
\{\theta_{i,j}\}_{i\in[t],j\in[Kt]}
. The quantities
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{Kt}
,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{Kt}
,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{Kt}
,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{Kt},\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{Kt}
are directly connected to the state evolution of the auxiliary AMP (see the remark at the end of Appendix
C.2
). Furthermore, the coefficients
\{\alpha_{i,j}\}_{j\in[i],i\in[Kt]}
,
\{\beta_{i,j}\}_{j\in[\lfloor(i-1)/K\rfloor+1],i\in[Kt]}
,
\{\gamma_{i}\}_{i\in[Kt]}
,
\{\theta_{i,j}\}_{i\in[t],j\in[Kt]}
allow for a useful (approximate) decomposition of the vectors
({\mathbf{Y}}^{\ell}{\mathbf{u}}^{t})_{\ell\in[K-1]}
, see the remark at the end of this section.

We start with the initialization

\tilde{U}_{1}:=U_{1},

(252)

where
U_{1}
satisfies (
251
), and we set

\begin{split}\tilde{\mu}_{1}&:=\lambda\epsilon,\quad(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{1})_{1,1}:=1,\quad(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{1})_{1,1}:=0,\quad(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{1})_{1,1}:=\bar{\kappa}_{1},\quad(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{1})_{1,1}:=\bar{\kappa}_{2},\\
\alpha_{1,1}&:=0,\quad\beta_{1,1}:=1,\quad\gamma_{1}:=0.\end{split}

(253)

Here and in what follows, we denote by
\{\bar{\kappa}_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
the sequence of free cumulants associated to
D
. The free cumulants can be recursively computed from the moments, see e.g.
[
91
, Section 2.5]
.

For
t\geq 1
, let us define

\displaystyle\tilde{U}_{K(t-1)+1+\ell}

\displaystyle:=\tilde{Z}_{K(t-1)+\ell}+\tilde{\mu}_{K(t-1)+\ell}X^{*}+\hskip-10.00002pt\sum_{j=1}^{K(t-1)+\ell}\hskip-5.0pt(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{K(t-1)+\ell})_{K(t-1)+\ell,j}\tilde{U}_{j},\,\ell\in[K-1],

(254)

\displaystyle\tilde{U}_{Kt+1}

\displaystyle:=g_{t+1}\Big(\mu_{t}X^{*}+\sum_{j=1}^{Kt}\theta_{t,j}\tilde{Z}_{j}\Big),

(255)

\displaystyle(\tilde{Z}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{Z}_{Kt})

\displaystyle\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{Kt})\mbox{ and independent of }X^{*},U_{1}.

(256)

We note that the function
g_{t+1}
in (
255
) is the AMP denoiser in (
249
). Let us also define

\displaystyle\tilde{\mu}_{K(t-1)+1+\ell}

\displaystyle=\lambda\mathbb{E}[\tilde{U}_{K(t-1)+1+\ell}X^{*}],

(257)

\displaystyle(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{K(t-1)+1+\ell})_{K(t-1)+1+\ell,j}

\displaystyle=(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{K(t-1)+1+\ell})_{j,K(t-1)+1+\ell}=\mathbb{E}[\tilde{U}_{K(t-1)+1+\ell}\tilde{U}_{j}],

(258)

\displaystyle\hskip 140.00021ptj\in[K(t-1)+1+\ell],

\displaystyle(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{K(t-1)+1+\ell})_{K(t-1)+1+\ell,j}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\partial_{\tilde{Z}_{j}}\tilde{U}_{K(t-1)+1+\ell}],\quad j\in[K(t-1)+\ell],

(259)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{K(t-1)+1+\ell}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{K(t-1)+\ell}\bar{\kappa}_{j+1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{K(t-1)+1+\ell}^{j},

(260)

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{K(t-1)+1+\ell}=\sum_{j=0}^{2(K(t-1)+\ell)}

\displaystyle\bar{\kappa}_{j+2}\sum_{k=0}^{j}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{K(t-1)+1+\ell})^{k}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{K(t-1)+1+\ell}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{K(t-1)+1+\ell}^{\intercal})^{j-k}.

(261)

Now, we obtain
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{K(t-1)+1},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{K(t-1)+1},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{K(t-1)+1},\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{K(t-1)+1},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{K(t-1)+1}
by setting
\ell=0
in (
257
)–(
261
) (and by using the initialization (
253
) for
t=1
). This allows us to define
\tilde{U}_{K(t-1)+2}
by setting
\ell=1
in (
254
). Next, we obtain
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{K(t-1)+2}
,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{K(t-1)+2}
,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{K(t-1)+2}
,
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{K(t-1)+2}
,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{K(t-1)+2}
by setting
\ell=1
in (
257
)–(
261
). This allows us to define
\tilde{U}_{K(t-1)+2}
by setting
\ell=2
in (
254
). We iterate this procedure until we have obtained
(
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{K(t-1)+\ell}
,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{K(t-1)+\ell}
,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{K(t-1)+\ell}
,
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{K(t-1)+\ell}
,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{K(t-1)+\ell}
)
ℓ∈[K]
and
(\tilde{U}_{K(t-1)+1+\ell})_{\ell\in[K-1]}
.
We note that, for any
i\geq 1
,
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{i}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}
are the top left sub-matrices of
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{i+1}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i+1}
, respectively.

At this point, for
\ell\in[K-1]
, we compute the quantities
\{\alpha_{K(t-1)+1+\ell,j}\}_{j\in[K(t-1)+\ell]}
,
\{\beta_{K(t-1)+1+\ell,j}\}_{j\in[t]}
,
\gamma_{K(t-1)+1+\ell}
as

\displaystyle\alpha_{K(t-1)+1+\ell,j}

\displaystyle=\delta_{K(t-1)+\ell,j}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\not\equiv 1({\rm mod}\,K)\end{subarray}}^{K(t-1)+\ell}\alpha_{i,j}\,(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{K(t-1)+\ell})_{K(t-1)+\ell,i},\quad j\in[K(t-1)+\ell],

(262)

\displaystyle\beta_{K(t-1)+1+\ell,j}

\displaystyle=(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{K(t-1)+\ell})_{K(t-1)+\ell,K(j-1)+1}+\hskip-5.0pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\not\equiv 1({\rm mod}\,K)\end{subarray}}^{K(t-1)+\ell}\hskip-7.5pt\beta_{i,j}\,(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{K(t-1)+\ell})_{K(t-1)+\ell,i},\,\,j\in[t],

(263)

\displaystyle\gamma_{K(t-1)+1+\ell}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mu}_{K(t-1)+\ell}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\not\equiv 1({\rm mod}\,K)\end{subarray}}^{K(t-1)+\ell}(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{K(t-1)+\ell})_{K(t-1)+\ell,i}\gamma_{i}.

(264)

In (
262
),
\delta_{i,j}
denotes the Kronecker symbol (
\delta_{i,j}=1
if
i=j
and
0
otherwise), and
\alpha_{i,j}
is assumed to be
0
if
j\geq i
; in (
263
),
\beta_{i,j}
is assumed to be
0
if
j>\lceil(i-1)/K\rceil
.

Recall that
\{c_{i}\}_{i=1}^{K}
are the coefficients of the polynomial
{\mathbf{J}}({\mathbf{Y}})
(in
{\mathbf{Y}}
), i.e.,
{\mathbf{J}}({\mathbf{Y}})=\sum_{i=1}^{K}c_{i}{\mathbf{Y}}^{i}
.
Finally, we are ready to express
\mu_{t}
,
\{\theta_{t,j}\}_{j\in[Kt]}
:

\displaystyle\mu_{t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{K}c_{i}\Big(\tilde{\mu}_{K(t-1)+i}+\sum_{k=1}^{K(t-1)+i}\gamma_{k}\,(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{K(t-1)+i})_{K(t-1)+i,k}\Big),

(265)

\displaystyle\theta_{t,j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{K}c_{i}\Big(\delta_{K(t-1)+i,j}+\sum_{k=1}^{K(t-1)+i}\alpha_{k,j}\,(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{K(t-1)+i})_{K(t-1)+i,k}\Big),\quad j\in[Kt].

(266)

As before,
\alpha_{i,j}
is assumed to be
0
if
j\geq i
. This allows us to define
\tilde{U}_{Kt+1}
via (
255
) and, after setting
\beta_{Kt+1,t+1}=1
,
\beta_{Kt+1,j}=0
for all
j\in[t]
,
\alpha_{Kt+1,j}=0
for all
j\in[Kt+1]
and
\gamma_{Kt+1}=0
, the definition of the state evolution recursion is complete.

From the state evolution recursion defined above, we can derive the Onsager coefficients
\{{\sf c}_{t,j}\}_{j\in[t]}
as

{\sf c}_{t,j}=\sum_{i=1}^{K}c_{i}\sum_{k=1}^{K(t-1)+i}\beta_{k,j}\,(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{K(t-1)+i})_{K(t-1)+i,k},\quad j\in[t].

(267)

At this point, we are ready to present our result concerning the characterization of the iterates of the AMP algorithm (
249
), with Onsager coefficients given by (
267
), in the high-dimensional limit
N\to\infty
: we prove that the convergence (
139
) holds, where
\mu_{t}
is given by (
265
) and
W_{t}=\sum_{j=1}^{Kt}\theta_{t,j}\tilde{Z}_{j}
, with
\{\theta_{t,j},\tilde{Z}_{j}\}_{j\in[Kt]}
described by the recursion above. Equivalently
[
48
, Corollary 7.21]
, the convergence can be expressed in terms of pseudo-Lipschitz test functions. A function
\psi\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}
is
pseudo-Lipschitz of order
2
, denoted by
\psi\in\mathrm{PL}(2)
, if there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\|\psi({\mathbf{x}})-\psi({\mathbf{y}})\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(1+\|{\mathbf{x}}\|_{2}+\|{\mathbf{y}}\|_{2}\Big)\|{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{y}}\|_{2},

for all
{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

Theorem 2

(State evolution of the BAMP)
.

Let
{\mathbf{Y}}
be given by (
17
) and which verifies Hypothesis
1
, and let
{\mathbf{J}}({\mathbf{Y}})=\sum_{i=1}^{K}c_{i}{\mathbf{Y}}^{i}
. Consider the AMP algorithm (
249
), with initialization (
251
), Onsager coefficients
\{{\sf c}_{t,j}\}_{j\in[t]}
given by (
267
) and where, for
t\geq 1
,
g_{t+1}
is continuously differentiable and Lipschitz. Then, the following limit holds almost surely for any
\mathrm{PL}(2)
function
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{2t+2}\to\mathbb{R}
, for
t\geq 1
as
N\to\infty
:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\leq N}\psi(u_{i}^{1},\ldots,u_{i}^{t+1},f_{i}^{1},\ldots,f_{i}^{t},X^{*}_{i})\to\mathbb{\mathbb{E}}\,\psi(U_{1},\ldots,U_{t+1},F_{1},\ldots,F_{t},X^{*}).

(268)

Equivalently, as
N\to\infty
, the joint empirical distribution of
({\mathbf{u}}^{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{u}}^{t+1},{\mathbf{f}}^{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{f}}^{t},{\mathbf{X}}^{*})
converges almost surely in Wasserstein-2 distance to
(U_{1},\ldots,U_{t+1},F_{1},\ldots,F_{t},X^{*})
. Here, for
i\in[t]
,
U_{i+1}=g_{i+1}(F_{t})
and
(F_{1},\ldots,F_{t})=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t}X^{*}+(W_{1},\ldots,W_{t})
, with
W_{t}=\sum_{j=1}^{Kt}\theta_{t,j}\tilde{Z}_{j}
and where
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{t}
can be computed via (
265
),
\{\theta_{t,j}\}_{j\in[Kt]}
via (
266
) and
\{Z_{j}\}_{j\in[Kt]}
is given by (
256
).

The proof of Theorem
2
is deferred to Appendix
C.3
. A few remarks are now in order.
First, we highlight that (
268
) directly implies a high-dimensional characterization of the performance of the AMP (
249
). In fact, by taking the pseudo-Lipschitz functions
\psi(U_{t+1},X^{*})=(U_{t+1}-X^{*})^{2}
,
\psi(U_{t+1},X^{*})=U_{t+1}\cdot X^{*}
and
\psi(U_{t+1},X^{*})=(U_{t+1})^{2}
, we obtain the limit mean-square error and overlap of the AMP iterates as

\begin{split}\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{2N^{2}}\mathbb{E}\|{\mathbf{X}}^{*}({\mathbf{X}}^{*})^{\intercal}-{\mathbf{u}}^{t}({\mathbf{u}}^{t})^{\intercal}\|_{\rm F}^{2}&=\frac{1}{2}\big(1-2\Big(\mathbb{E}[U_{t}\cdot X^{*}]\Big)^{2}+(\mathbb{E}[(U_{t})^{2}])^{2}\big),\\
\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{|\langle{\mathbf{X}}^{*},{\mathbf{u}}^{t}\rangle|}{\|{\mathbf{u}}^{t}\|\cdot\|{\mathbf{X}}^{*}\|}&=\frac{|\mathbb{E}[U_{t}\cdot X^{*}]|}{\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[(U_{t})^{2}]}}.\end{split}

(269)

Next, note that Theorem
2
holds for any family of denoisers
\{g_{t+1}\}_{t\geq 1}
, subject to some mild regularity requirement. A natural choice is to pick the posterior mean

g_{t+1}(f)=\mathbb{E}[U_{*}\mid F_{t}=f].

(270)

Such a choice requires estimating the state evolution parameters
\mu_{t}
,
\{\theta_{t,j}\}_{j\in[Kt]}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{Kt}
. These parameters, as well as the Onsager coefficients (
267
), can be estimated consistently from the data. To do so, first we obtain
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{Kt}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{Kt}
by replacing expectations with empirical averages in (
258
) and (
259
), respectively. Next, we compute
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{Kt}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{Kt}
by plugging in such estimates in (
260
) and (
261
), respectively. Having done that, we obtain
\{\alpha_{K(t-1)+1+\ell,j}\}_{j\in[K(t-1)+\ell],\ell\in[K-1]}
,
\{\beta_{K(t-1)+1+\ell,j}\}_{j\in[t],\ell\in[K-1]}
,
\{\gamma_{K(t-1)+1+\ell}\}_{\ell\in[K-1]}
via (
262
)–(
264
). Finally,
\mu_{t}
,
\{\theta_{t,j}\}_{j\in[Kt]}
and
\{{\sf c}_{t,j}\}_{j\in[t]}
can be computed from (
265
), (
266
) and (
267
), respectively.

As a final remark, we provide an interpretation of the coefficients
\{\alpha_{i,j}\}
,
\{\beta_{i,j}\}
,
\{\gamma_{i}\}
. As a by-product of the argument proving Theorem
2
, we will show that, for
\ell\in[K-1]
, (cf. (
298
)–(
299
))

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}

\displaystyle\frac{\|{\mathbf{Y}}^{\ell}{\mathbf{u}}^{t}\hskip-1.99997pt-\hskip-1.99997pt\sum_{j=1}^{K(t-1)+\ell}\hskip-1.99997pt\alpha_{K(t-1)+1+\ell,j}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}^{j}\hskip-1.99997pt-\hskip-1.99997pt\sum_{j=1}^{t}\hskip-1.99997pt\beta_{K(t-1)+1+\ell,j}{\mathbf{u}}^{j}\hskip-1.99997pt-\hskip-1.99997pt\gamma_{K(t-1)+1+\ell}{\mathbf{X}}^{*}\|^{2}}{N}=0.

(271)

This formalizes the fact that
{\mathbf{Y}}^{\ell}{\mathbf{u}}^{t}
can be approximately expressed as a linear combination of
(i)
the past iterates
\{{\mathbf{u}}^{j}\}_{j\in[t]}
,
(ii)
the signal
{\mathbf{X}}^{*}
, plus
(iii)
independent Gaussian noise (represented by the
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}^{j}
’s). The quantities
\{\alpha_{i,j}\}
,
\{\beta_{i,j}\}
,
\{\gamma_{i}\}
represent the coefficients of this linear combination. The characterization (
271
) allows to subtract from
{\mathbf{J}}({\mathbf{Y}}){\mathbf{u}}^{k}
just the right Onsager terms, so that this difference equals a component in the direction of the signal (whose size is captured by
\mu_{t}
) plus independent Gaussian noise (given by the linear combination of the
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}^{j}
’s via the coefficients
\{\theta_{i,j}\}
).

10.3
AMP-AP: AMP with Alternating Posteriors

As discussed above, the derivation of the Onsager coefficients for BAMP involves approximating vectors of the form
\{{\mathbf{Y}}^{\ell}{\mathbf{u}}^{t}\}_{\ell\leq K-1}
. This fact suggests an alternative choice for the denoisers of the AMP in
[
45
]
. For each batch of
K
iterations, we pick linear denoisers in the first
K-1
of them, as this allows to construct the vectors
\{{\mathbf{Y}}^{\ell}{\mathbf{u}}^{t}\}_{\ell\leq K-1}
; then, in the
K
-th iteration, we pick the posterior mean using
all
the past iterates, as this – in principle – allows to assemble the vectors
\{{\mathbf{Y}}^{\ell}{\mathbf{u}}^{t}\}_{\ell\leq K-1}
to obtain the quantity
{\mathbf{J}}({\mathbf{Y}}){\mathbf{u}}^{t}
. We refer to this algorithm as AMP with Alternating Posteriors (AMP-AP). In formulas, AMP-AP is given by a slight generalization of (
138
), where the entry-wise denoiser function depends on all past iterates, as follows:

\begin{split}h_{t+1}(f_{i}^{1},f_{i}^{2},\ldots,f_{i}^{t})&=f_{i}^{t},\qquad\mbox{for }t\not\equiv 1\,\,\,(\mbox{mod }K),\\
h_{t+1}(f_{i}^{1},f_{i}^{2},\ldots,f_{i}^{t})&=\mathbb{E}[X\mid f_{i}^{1},f_{i}^{2},\ldots,f_{i}^{t}],\qquad\mbox{for }t\equiv 1\,\,\,(\mbox{mod }K).\end{split}

(272)

We note that AMP-AP does not require the coefficients of the polynomial
{\mathbf{J}}({\mathbf{Y}})
. In fact, it leaves to the posterior mean denoiser the task of learning them from the data. As such, it provides an efficient alternative to our proposed BAMP.

11
Numerics

For all experiments in this section, random instances of
{\mathbf{Y}}
are generated according to the model (
17
). The noise matrices
{\mathbf{Z}}={\mathbf{O}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{D}}{\mathbf{O}}
are generated by first drawing
N
i.i.d. eigenvalues
(D_{i})_{i\leq N}
according to the density (
29
), or (
34
) (with
\mu=\gamma=0
), and then multiplying from left and right the diagonal matrix of eigenvalues
{\mathbf{D}}
by a random Haar distributed orthogonal matrix
{\mathbf{O}}
sampled independently for each realization. As mentioned at the end of Section
5.4
, the results are expected to be the same if we were to draw
{\mathbf{Z}}
according to the harder to sample
4
4

4

This can be done using the Dyson Brownian motion, see
[
101
]
.
measure (
20
).

11.1
Spectral properties of the pre-processed matrix
{\mathbf{J}}({\mathbf{Y}})

Figure 5:
Ranked eigenvalues of the data matrix
{\mathbf{Y}}
(orange) and the optimally pre-processed matrix
{\mathbf{J}}({\mathbf{Y}})
(blue) for
N=4000\ \mbox{for (left)}\ \lambda=2\ \mbox{and (right)}\ \lambda=5
. The gap between the largest detached eigenvalue on the extreme right and the second highest one is much bigger for the pre-processed matrix. Moreover, all the eigenvalues of
{\mathbf{J}}({\mathbf{Y}})
in its non-informative bulk are negative.

(a)
Empirical spectral density of
{\mathbf{Y}}
. The largest, informative, eigenvalue is emphasized.

(b)
The function
J(x)=\mu\lambda x-\gamma\lambda^{2}x^{2}+\gamma\lambda x^{3}
with
(\mu=0,\gamma(0)=16/27)
is used to optimally pre-process the (eigenvalues of the) data
{\mathbf{Y}}
and obtain
{\mathbf{J}}({\mathbf{Y}})
. The dashed curve indicates
0
. By comparison with the plots (a) above, we understand that the noise bulk will be pushed to negative values, while the spike towards the right, which results in a “cleaning” effect.

(c)
Empirical spectral density of the pre-processed matrix
{\mathbf{J}}({\mathbf{Y}})
. The largest eigenvalue is emphasized and well separated from the negative bulk by the application of
J(x)
.

Figure 6:
Effect of the optimal pre-processing
J(x)
on the eigenvalues of
{\mathbf{Y}}
. All experiments are for the most structured noise ensemble
(\mu=0,\gamma(0)=16/27)
and
N=4000
. The left column corresponds to
\lambda=2
, while the right column to
\lambda=5
.

Let us discuss the effect on the spectrum of
{\mathbf{Y}}
that has the application of the optimal pre-processing function
J(\cdot)
; clearly, this function does not influence the eigenvectors of
{\mathbf{Y}}
which therefore has the same basis as
{\mathbf{J}}({\mathbf{Y}})
. From Figures
5
and
6
, the effect is clear: the function
J
(Figure
6
, middle plots (b)) “cleans” the eigenvalues of the data
{\mathbf{Y}}
(Figure
6
, upper plots (a)) by shifting the non-informative bulk eigenvalues of
{\mathbf{Y}}
to negative values, while the largest, informative, eigenvalue is further separated from the bulk. This results in the histograms (Figure
6
, lower plots (c)) for the processed data
{\mathbf{J}}({\mathbf{Y}})
. It thus becomes much easier to distinguish the informative eigenvalue, which may be of interest for smaller instances where the finite-size effects are stronger.

11.2
BAMP and AMP-AP improve over the existing AMP and match the replica prediction for the MMSE, and empirical universality of the rotational invariance assumption

The plots of Figure
7
consider the quartic ensemble discussed in Section
5.3
for three values of the parameter
\mu
, namely,
\mu\in\{0,0.5,1\}
(recall
\gamma=\gamma(\mu)
is fixed by relation (
32
)), and the power six ensemble (
33
). The signal has Rademacher prior, i.e., i.i.d. entries
X_{i}^{*}\sim\frac{1}{2}(\delta_{1}+\delta_{-1})
. The estimators of the spike
{\mathbf{X}}^{*}{\mathbf{X}}^{*\intercal}
are compared in terms of the MSE (
y
-axis) achieved at the fixed point, as a function of the SNR
\lambda
(
x
-axis). All algorithms are run for
N=8000
and the results are averaged over
n_{\rm trials}=50
independent trials; the state evolution recursions (and the replica prediction as well) correspond to
N\to\infty
. We compare the following inference procedures:

•

In black, we plot the replica prediction (
100
), obtained as the fixed point of (
94
)–(
99
).

•

In red, we plot the performance of the BAMP algorithm described in Section
10
, where
g_{t+1}
is the posterior mean denoiser (
270
). More specifically, the red line corresponds to the fixed point of the MSE given by the state evolution recursion discussed in Section
10.2
(cf. (
269
)), and the red stars denote the MSE obtained by running the BAMP algorithm (
249
).

•

In blue, we plot the performance of the AMP proposed in
[
45
]
. More specifically, the blue line corresponds to the fixed point of the MSE (
141
) obtained by choosing the posterior mean denoiser with a single-step memory term (
140
). The blue diamonds denote the MSE obtained by running the AMP (
