) with this single-step denoiser.

•

The ochre squares are MSE values obtained by the AMP of
[
110
]
(without the pre-processing of
\mathbf{Y}
), which employs a full memory posterior mean denoiser:

h_{t+1}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{t})=\mathbb{E}[X^{*}\mid(F_{1},\ldots,F_{t})=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{t})]\,.

(273)

•

Finally, the green triangles denote the performance of BAMP when the uniformly distributed matrix
{\mathbf{O}}
(appearing in the spectral decomposition of the noise
{\mathbf{Z}}
) is replaced by the product of the Hadamard-Walsh matrix and a diagonal matrix with i.i.d. Rademacher entries as in
[
42
]
.

(a)
Quartic potential with
\mu=1
.

(b)
Quartic potential with
\mu=0.5
.

(c)
Quartic potential with
\mu=0
.

(d)
Power six potential with
\xi=27/80
.

Figure 7:
Performance comparison between the replica prediction for the MMSE (in black), the proposed BAMP (in red), and the existing AMPs
[
45
,
110
]
(in blue and green). BAMP matches the Bayes-optimal MSE predicted via the replica method, and it outperforms the existing AMP when the noise is not Gaussian. This improvement is more evident as the noise distribution gets further from a Wigner distribution.

We note that all algorithms converge rapidly:
10
iterations are sufficient to reach the corresponding fixed points. A few remarks concerning the numerical results displayed in Figure
7
are now in order:

•

For both the quartic and the sestic potential, the fixed point of the BAMP state evolution (in red) matches the replica prediction (in black). This is a strong numerical evidence supporting our conjecture that the proposed BAMP algorithm is Bayes-optimal. These theoretical curves for
N\to\infty
are also remarkably close to the MSE achieved by the BAMP algorithm (
249
) at
N=8000
.

•

When
\mu=0
in the quartic potential, i.e., the noise is sufficiently far from being independent Gaussian, there is a clear performance gap between our proposed BAMP (in red) and the existing AMP algorithms
[
45
,
110
]
(single-step denoiser in blue, and multi-step in green). As predicted by our theory, this gap is reduced for
\mu=0.5
, and all curves collapse for
\mu=1
.
An even greater gap occurs when we consider the power six ensemble in Figure
7d
, which is “further” from the Wigner ensemble.

•

Finally, we note that the BAMP algorithm exhibits a numerical instability for low SNR. More specifically, when
\mu=0
in the quartic potential and
\lambda=2.3
, 5 out of the 50 trials of the iteration in (
39
) do not reach the fixed point of state evolution (and are therefore discarded). Furthermore, by inspecting Figure
7c
, one notices that the curve representing the BAMP state evolution detaches from the replica prediction as the SNR gets smaller than
2.3
. As expected, considering an initialization closer to the fixed point mitigates the issue. This numerical instability is likely due to BAMP’s state evolution corresponding to the recursion of an auxiliary AMP that
triples
the number of iterations. This leads to an amplification of numerical errors. The same phenomenon occurs with the power six potential, where the number of iterations is multiplied by five. The problem is again mitigated by providing an initialization close to the fixed point. Nevertheless, for
\lambda=2.15
and
\lambda=2.3
respectively 2 and 1 BAMP iterations do not reach the fix point of state evolution and are discarded.

Let us re-emphasize that all these results hold in the Bayesian-optimal setting where all hyper-parameters of the model are known and optimally used. In practical situations this may not be the case. In particular the statistical properties of the correlated noise
{\mathbf{Z}}
may be only partially known, preventing one to obtain the coefficients
(c_{k})
defining the optimal pre-processing of the data
{\mathbf{J}}({\mathbf{Y}})=\sum_{k\leq K}c_{k}{\mathbf{Y}}^{k}
as done in Section
9
. In Appendix
B
we provide a learning procedure based on expectation maximization to overcome this issue and which can be of help to practitioners aiming at using BAMP in more realistic situations. Its testing is left for future work.

(a)
Quartic potential with
\mu=0
.

(b)
Power six potential with
\xi=27/80
.

Figure 8:
Performance comparison between the replica prediction for the MMSE (in black), AMP-AP (in blue), and BAMP run when the noise matrix is not rotationally invariant (black, red, ochre and green symbols). AMP-AP matches the Bayes-optimal MSE predicted via the replica method and, hence, it provides an efficient alternative to BAMP. Furthermore, BAMP displays a remarkable universality behavior, in the sense that its performance is close to the state evolution prediction even when the eigenbasis of the noise is taken from the covariance matrix of datasets commonly employed in practice.

AMP-AP provides an algorithmic alternative that does not require the computation of the coefficients
(c_{k})
. Its performance for the quartic potential with
\mu=0
(left plot) and for the sestic potential (right plot) is represented in blue in Figure
8
. More specifically, the blue stars denote the MSE obtained by running the algorithm (
138
) with the denoisers given by (
272
), and the blue curve is the corresponding state evolution. We remark the excellent agreement with the minimum MSE predicted by the replica formula. In Figure
8
, we also run BAMP when the noise matrix is
not rotationally invariant
, but its eigenbasis comes from the covariance matrix of datasets commonly used in computer vision and quantitative genetics. In particular, we report the results for two CIFAR-10 classes (“plane” and “cat”), and two GTEx datasets (“muscle skeletal” and “skin sun exposed lower leg”)
[
74
]
. For the two CIFAR-10 classes, we have
N=1024
. The two GTEx datasets are matrices of 56200 rows and, respectively, 803 and 701 columns; we pick the first 8000 rows and construct a covariance matrix (hence,
N=8000
). Again, the BAMP performance matches the replica predictions, thus providing an empirical confirmation of the universality of our results.

Figure 9:
Performance comparison between the replica prediction for the MMSE (in black), BAMP (in red), AMP-AP (in green), and the existing AMPs
[
45
,
110
]
(in blue and ochre). We consider a quartic potential with
\mu=0
and a signal with a sparse Rademacher prior with
\rho=0.3
. Once again, both BAMP and AMP-AP match the replica prediction and improve upon previously proposed algorithms.

Finally, in Figure
9
, we consider the quartic potential with
\mu=0
and a signal having a sparse Rademacher prior, i.e., i.i.d. entries
X_{i}^{*}\sim(1-\rho)\delta_{0}+\frac{\rho}{2}\delta_{-1/\sqrt{\rho}}+\frac{\rho}{2}\delta_{1/\sqrt{\rho}}
. We pick
\rho=0.3
. As in the previous cases, BAMP (red) and AMP-AP (green) meet the MMSE predicted via the replica method (black), and they outperform the AMPs previously proposed in
[
45
,
110
]
(blue and ochre). All algorithms are run for
N=8000
, except the point
\lambda=3.8
for which we use
N=12000
in order to improve the convergence to state evolution.

Taken all together, the numerical results of Figures
7
-
9
provide a clear empirical confirmation of the (Bayes-)optimality of the proposed algorithms, as well as of the universality of BAMP.

Appendix A
Approximation of non-polynomial potentials

In this appendix we will argue that the general strategy presented to study the inference task associated to noise coming from random matrix ensembles with polynomial potentials can be used to approximate the MMSE of noise ensembles with general analytic potentials by considering a proper sequence of polynomials that converges point-wise. In the following argument we will assume that:

(i)

the potential
V:\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
is analytic,

(ii)

there is a constant
C>0
such that, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
, we have that
V\geq Cx^{2}/2
,

(iii)

and the coordinates of
\mathbf{X}^{*}
are i.i.d. of density
P_{X}
with bounded support.

Although condition (iii) can be weakened at the cost of some extra technicalities, here we will include it to keep the presentation more simple.

By condition (i), the potentials considered are analytic. Then, there is some sequence
(c_{k})_{k\geq 1}
such that, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

V(x)=\sum_{k\geq 1}c_{k}x^{k}.

Let
p\geq 1
and define
V_{p}:\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
according to
V_{p}(x):=\sum_{k\leq p}c_{k}x^{k}
. In this way, we will define
\mathbf{Z}_{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
to be a random matrix of probability distribution

\displaystyle dP_{Z_{p}}(\mathbf{Z}_{p})=C_{V_{p}}\exp\Big(-\frac{N}{2}{\rm Tr}V_{p}(\mathbf{Z}_{p})\Big)\prod_{i\leq j}dZ_{p,ij}\,;

(274)

where, as before,
C_{V_{p}}>0
is just a normalizing constant. Also define a new data matrix
\mathbf{Y}_{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
according to
\mathbf{Y}_{p}:=\frac{\lambda}{N}\mathbf{P}^{*}+\mathbf{Z}_{p}
. Here we will introduce the posterior measures

\displaystyle dP^{(p)}_{X\mid Y_{p}}(\mathbf{x}\mid{\mathbf{Y}}_{p})\!=\!\frac{dP_{X}(\mathbf{x})}{Z_{p}(\mathbf{Y}_{p})}\exp\Big(\!-\frac{N}{2}{\rm Tr}V_{p}\Big({\mathbf{Y}}_{p}-\frac{\lambda}{N}\mathbf{P}\Big)\!\Big)

(275)

and

\displaystyle d{P^{\prime}}^{(p)}_{X\mid Y}(\mathbf{x}\mid{\mathbf{Y}})\!=\!\frac{dP_{X}(\mathbf{x})}{Z^{\prime}_{p}(\mathbf{Y})}\exp\Big(\!-\frac{N}{2}{\rm Tr}V_{p}\Big({\mathbf{Y}}-\frac{\lambda}{N}\mathbf{P}\Big)\!\Big);

(276)

with
Z_{p}(\mathbf{Y}_{p}),Z^{\prime}_{p}(\mathbf{Y})>0
normalization constants. Notice that
P^{(p)}_{X\mid Y_{p}}(\cdot)
corresponds to the Bayes-optimal posterior of data with noise
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
and
{P^{\prime}}^{(p)}_{X\mid Y}(\cdot)
is the mismatched posterior obtained from a signal generated with noise from an ensemble of potential
V
but wrongly modeled as having noise from potential
V_{p}
. The free entropies associated with these posteriors will then be
F_{N}^{(p)}:=\mathbb{E}\ln Z_{p}(\mathbf{Y}_{p})
and
{F^{\prime}}_{N}^{(p)}:=\mathbb{E}\ln Z^{\prime}_{p}(\mathbf{Y})
. And finally, the associated mutual information between data and signal for the first of the two posteriors, which is Bayes-optimal, will be given by

I_{p}({\mathbf{P}}^{*};{\mathbf{Y}}_{p}):=-F^{(p)}_{N}({\mathbf{Y}}_{p})-\frac{N}{2}\mathbb{E}{\rm Tr}V_{p}(\mathbf{Z}_{p}).

In this appendix we will argue that

\lim_{p\to\infty}\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}|I({\mathbf{P}}^{*};{\mathbf{Y}})-I_{p}({\mathbf{P}}^{*};{\mathbf{Y}}_{p})|=0.

(277)

By including side information of the form
\mathbf{\tilde{Y}}=\tilde{\lambda}\mathbf{X}_{*}+\mathbf{\tilde{Z}}
with
\tilde{\lambda}>0
and
\mathbf{\tilde{Z}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
a standard Gaussian vector, the magnetization
m
of both models can be obtained as a derivative with respect to
\tilde{\lambda}
of each asymptotic mutual information. The strategy to derive the free entropy limit in the main text can be easily adapted to include this side. Furthermore, if
\tilde{\lambda}
is taken to be small (i.e., the side information has a low signal-to-noise ratio in some proper sense), the asymptotic value of
m
is not modified by this side information. See for example
[
15
, Section 5.1.1]
, for more details on this strategy. Finally, because the free entropies are convex functions of
\tilde{\lambda}
, equation (
277
) implies that the asymptotic values of the magnetization
m
of both models coincide when
p
goes to infinity whenever the signal-to-noise ratio is not taking a critical value. This then means that the MMSE of the model with noise of potential
V
differs with respect to the one of the model with noise of potential
V_{p}
by a term that is vanishing in
p
. This therefore justifies the fact of studying only models with polynomial potentials.

In the rest of the section we will justify (
277
). To see that this should hold, we will first bound

\begin{split}\frac{1}{N}|I({\mathbf{P}}^{*};{\mathbf{Y}})-I_{p}({\mathbf{P}}^{*};{\mathbf{Y}}_{p})|&\leq\frac{1}{N}\Big|F_{N}-{F^{\prime}}^{(p)}_{N}+\frac{N}{2}{\rm Tr}(V({\mathbf{Z}})-V_{p}({\mathbf{Z}}))\Big|\\
&\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,+\frac{1}{N}\Big|I_{p}({\mathbf{P}}^{*};{\mathbf{Y}}_{p})+{F^{\prime}}^{(p)}_{N}+\frac{N}{2}\mathbb{E}{\rm Tr}V_{p}(\mathbf{Z}_{p})\Big|.\end{split}

(278)

For bounding the first term on the right of (
278
) we will introduce, for every
t\in[0,1]
, the interpolating measure of mean
\langle\cdot\rangle_{t}
corresponding to the Hamiltonian

H_{N,t}(\mathbf{X})=-\frac{N}{2}{\rm Tr}\,V_{t}\Big(\mathbf{Y}-\frac{\lambda}{N}\mathbf{P}\Big);

where
V_{t}(x):=V_{p}(x)+tE_{p}(x)
and
E_{p}(x):=\sum_{k\geq p+1}c_{k}x^{k}
. Let
F_{N,t}
be the free entropy associated to
H_{N,t}
and define
G_{t}:=-F_{N,t}-N/2{\rm Tr}V_{t}({\mathbf{Z}})
. Clearly, we have that
G_{0}=-{F^{\prime}}^{(p)}_{N}-N/2{\rm Tr}V_{p}({\mathbf{Z}})
and
G_{1}=-F_{N}-N/2{\rm Tr}V({\mathbf{Z}})
. Notice that for all
t\in[0,1]
,

\frac{1}{N}\frac{dG_{t}}{dt}=\,\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\Big\langle{\rm Tr}\,E_{p}\big(\mathbf{Y}-\frac{\lambda}{N}\mathbf{P}\big)\Big\rangle_{t}-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}{\rm Tr}\,E_{p}\big({\mathbf{Z}}\big).

We would now like to see that the absolute value of the right hand side of the last equation is
o_{p}(1)
. For this, denote by
D_{1},\dots D_{N}
the eigenvalues of
{\mathbf{Z}}
ordered from largest to smallest. Likewise, denote by
\tilde{D}_{1},\dots,\tilde{D}_{N}
the ones of
{\mathbf{Y}}-\lambda/N{\mathbf{P}}
. By Weyl’s interlacing inequalities we have that

D_{i}\leq\tilde{D}_{i}\leq D_{i-2}\,\,\,\,\,\mbox{ for all }i=3,\dots,N.

This means that, if we denote (for
i=2,\dots,N
)
\delta_{i}:=D_{i}-D_{i-1}
, we then have that, for all
i=3,\dots,N
,

|E_{p}(\tilde{D}_{i})-E_{p}(D_{i})|\leq(\delta_{i-1}+\delta_{i})E^{\prime}_{p}(\xi_{i}),

for some
D_{i}\leq\xi_{i}\leq D_{i-2}
. By condition (ii) above, we know that the limiting distribution
\rho
of the eigenvalues of
\mathbf{Z}
and the distribution
\rho_{p}
of the ones of
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
are, for large enough
p
, both contained in the compact interval
[-2/C,2/C]
. This is so because condition (ii) implies that the potentials
V
and
V_{p}
are more confining than
Cx^{2}/2
(see
[
101
, Section 5.2]
for more details). Thus, under
\langle\cdot\rangle_{t}
, by this and condition (iii) the eigenvalues of
\mathbf{Y}-\lambda/N\mathbf{P}
and
{\mathbf{Z}}
are contained in the interval
[-(1/C+2\lambda),1/C+2\lambda]
. On
[-(1/C+2\lambda),1/C+2\lambda]
we will have
|E^{\prime}_{p}|\leq\epsilon_{p}
for some vanishing sequence
(\epsilon_{p})_{p\geq 1}
. From which we get that, for all
i=3,\dots,N
,

|E_{p}(\tilde{D}_{i})-E_{p}(D_{i})|\leq(\delta_{i-1}+\delta_{i})\epsilon_{p}

Moreover, on the interval
[-(1/C+2\lambda),1/C+2\lambda]
, we have that there is another vanishing sequence
(\epsilon^{\prime}_{p})_{p\geq 1}
such that
|E_{p}|\leq\epsilon^{\prime}_{p}
. From this we have

\begin{split}\Big|{\rm Tr}\Big[E_{p}\big(\mathbf{Y}-\frac{\lambda}{N}\mathbf{P}\big)-E_{p}\big({\mathbf{Z}}\big)\Big]\Big|&\leq|E_{p}(\tilde{D}_{1})|+|E_{p}(\tilde{D}_{2})|+|E_{p}(D_{1})|\\
&\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,+|E_{p}(D_{2})|+\sum_{i=3}^{N}|E_{p}(\tilde{D}_{i})-E_{p}(D_{i})|\\
&\leq 4\epsilon^{\prime}_{p}+\epsilon_{p}\sum_{i=3}^{N}(\delta_{i-1}+\delta_{i})\leq 4\epsilon^{\prime}_{p}+\frac{4\epsilon_{p}}{C}\xrightarrow{p\to\infty}0.\end{split}

From this we then conclude that
N^{-1}|G_{1}-G_{0}|=o_{p}(1)
which means that the first term on the right hand side of (
278
) is vanishing in
p
.

For the second term on the right hand side of (
278
) we will draw some of the conclusions from
[
6
, Theorem 2.6.1]
. If we define a functional
\Sigma(\cdot)
over the probability distributions on the line according to
\Sigma(\mu)=\int\int\ln|x-y|d\mu(x)d\mu(y)
if
\int\ln(|x|+1)d\mu(x)<\infty
and
\Sigma(\mu)=-\infty
otherwise, as a consequence of the theorem we have that the empirical eigenvalue measure of
\mathbf{Z}
obeys a large deviation principle of speed
N^{2}
and good rate function

I_{V}(\mu):=\begin{cases}\int V(x)d\mu(x)-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma(\mu)-c_{V}&\mbox{if }\int V(x)d\mu(x)<\infty\\
\infty&\mbox{o.w.};\end{cases}

where
c_{V}:=\inf_{\mu}\int V(x)d\mu(x)-1/2\Sigma(\mu)
. Similarly, the empirical eigenvalue measure of
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
obeys a large deviation principle of speed
N^{2}
and good rate function

I_{V_{p}}(\mu):=\begin{cases}\int V_{p}(x)d\mu(x)-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma(\mu)-c_{V_{p}}&\mbox{if }\int V_{p}(x)d\mu(x)<\infty\\
\infty&\mbox{o.w.};\end{cases}

with
c_{V_{p}}:=\inf_{\mu}\int V_{p}(x)d\mu(x)-1/2\Sigma(\mu)
. By
[
6
, Lemma 2.6.2]
, both
I_{V}
and
I_{V_{p}}
are strictly convex. By condition (ii), for finding the minimum of the rate functions, we can restrict the optimization problem to densities supported on the interval
[-1/C,1/C]
. Then, because
V_{p}\xrightarrow{p\to\infty}V
, we have that the minimizer of
I_{V_{p}}
has to converge to that of
I_{V}
. We then have that
\rho_{p}
converges point-wise to
\rho
. Finally, notice that, for general polynomial potentials, the function
f_{\rho}
defining the optimization problem that gives the limiting free entropy should be continuous with respect to
\rho
. Therefore, when the minimizer of
f_{\rho}
is unique, we should then have that the minimizer of
f_{\rho_{p}}
should approach it when
p
goes to infinity. Here we implicitly assumed that, if
p
is sufficiently large, the posterior
{P^{\prime}}^{(p)}_{X\mid Y}(\mathbf{x}\mid{\mathbf{Y}})
is replica symmetric. This then means that
\mbox{extr}_{\tau}f_{\rho_{p}}(\tau)\approx\mbox{extr}_{\tau}f_{\rho}(\tau)
. We then have that
N^{-1}|F^{(p)}_{N}-{F^{\prime}}^{(p)}_{N}|
is
o_{p}(1)
. Finally,
N^{-1}|\mathbb{E}{\rm Tr}V_{p}(\mathbf{Z}_{p})-\mathbb{E}{\rm Tr}V_{p}(\mathbf{Z})|
is also
o_{p}(1)
because of the convergence of
\rho_{p}
towards
\rho
. This means have that the second term in (
278
) is
o_{p}(1)
from which we conclude (
277
).

Appendix B
Learning the optimal pre-processing
{\mathbf{J}}({\mathbf{Y}})

Until now we have assumed that we are in the Bayesian-optimal setting where, in particular, the polynomial potential
V
defining the noise statistics is completely known and correctly exploited. As seen from section
9.2
, given a potential
V
we could deduce from the AdaTAP formalism an optimal polynomial

{\mathbf{J}}={\mathbf{J}}({\mathbf{Y}})=\sum_{k\leq K}c_{k}{\mathbf{Y}}^{k}

to pre-process the data
{\mathbf{Y}}
before using it in AMP. The Bayes-optimal case corresponds to matrix (
209
), i.e.,
{\mathbf{J}}=c_{1}{\mathbf{Y}}+c_{2}{\mathbf{Y}}^{2}+c_{3}{\mathbf{Y}}^{3}
with
{\mathbf{c}}=(\mu\lambda,-\gamma\lambda^{2},\gamma\lambda)
.

We here consider an extension of the previously derived AMP to a case where
V
is not known and therefore the optimal
{\mathbf{J}}
cannot be deduced by the AdaTAP approach as we did in Section
9.2
. What is known instead is an upper bound on the order of
V
. In the base-case model studied in details in the present paper the order is four. The procedure we propose below will not be tested numerically yet, but we believe it may be of interest to practitioners eager to improve the Bayes-optimal AMP for more practical settings than the specific ones studied here.

To directly learn the coefficients
(c_{k})_{k\leq K}
from the data, we propose to use an approach inspired by the expectation maximization (EM) algorithm, with a routine inside AMP performing the parameter estimation by maximizing the current estimate of the free entropy, i.e., of the log-likelihood of the observed data
\ln P({\mathbf{Y}}\mid{\mathbf{c}})
.

Assume that, at the AMP iterate
t
, the current estimate of the unknown coefficients
{\mathbf{c}}=(c_{k})_{k\leq K}
is
{\mathbf{c}}(t)=(c_{k}(t))_{k\leq K}
, the AMP estimate of the marginal means is
{\mathbf{m}}(t)
, and of the Onsager reaction term is
\bar{V}(t)
(which is related to the set of Onsager coefficients, see Section
10.2
). Let also the data matrix polynomial currently used by AMP be

{\mathbf{J}}(t):=\sum_{k\leq K}c_{k}(t){\mathbf{Y}}^{k}.

From the analysis of Section
9.4
we know that at the saddle point we can safely replace the Onsager reaction term
V_{i}
by
\bar{V}
in the AdaTAP equations. When this is plugged back into (
240
), this identity implies that also the following concentration is consistently valid:
\mathbb{E}(\tau_{i}-m_{i}^{2})=\tau_{i}-m_{i}^{2}
, which is also equal by exchangeability to
N^{-1}\sum_{i\leq N}\mathbb{E}(\tau_{i}-m_{i}^{2})
. Let us call
\bar{\chi}(t)
the AMP estimate of the variance
\mathbb{E}(\tau_{i}-m_{i}^{2})
. Applying these simplifications to the AMP iterates we get that the matrix
\boldsymbol{\Omega}(t):={\rm diag}({\mathbf{V}}(t)+({\boldsymbol{\tau}}(t)-{\mathbf{m}}(t)^{2})^{-1})
can be simplified as

\boldsymbol{\Omega}(t)=(\bar{V}(t)+\bar{\chi}(t)^{-1})I_{N}.

From section
9.1
the AdaTAP approximation to the free entropy at iterate
t
then reads, using these simplifications, as

\displaystyle\Phi_{N}(t,{\mathbf{c}}(t))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}{\mathbf{m}}(t)^{\intercal}{\mathbf{J}}(t){\mathbf{m}}(t)+\frac{1}{2}\ln\det\big(\boldsymbol{\Omega}(t)-{\mathbf{J}}(t)\big)-\frac{1}{2}\bar{V}(t)\sum_{i\leq N}m_{i}(t)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\bar{\chi}(t)

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt-\sum_{i\leq N}\ln\int dP_{X}(x)\exp\Big(\frac{1}{2}\bar{V}(t)x^{2}+\big(({\mathbf{J}}(t){\mathbf{m}}(t))_{i}-\bar{V}(t)m_{i}(t)\big)x\Big).

(279)

The free entropy
\Phi_{N}(t,{\mathbf{c}}(t))
is the current best approximation to the marginal log-likelihood of the data
\ln P({\mathbf{Y}}\mid{\mathbf{c}})
, which we thus aim at maximizing with respect to the unknown parameters, all other quantities being fixed at their current values:

\displaystyle\partial_{c_{k}}\Phi_{N}|_{t,{\mathbf{c}}(t)}

\displaystyle={\mathbf{m}}(t)^{\intercal}{\mathbf{Y}}^{k}\Big(\frac{1}{2}{\mathbf{m}}(t)-\eta({\mathbf{J}}(t),{\mathbf{m}}(t),\bar{V}(t))\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{1}{2}{\rm Tr}\big({\mathbf{Y}}^{k}(\boldsymbol{\Omega}(t)-{\mathbf{J}}(t))^{-1}\big),

(280)

where we used (
211
) and the notation
\eta({\mathbf{J}}(t),{\mathbf{m}}(t),\bar{V}(t))=(\eta_{i}({\mathbf{J}}(t),{\mathbf{m}}(t),\bar{V}(t))_{i\leq N}
. Because
{\mathbf{J}}
is diagonalizable in the same basis as the data
{\mathbf{Y}}
, the eigenvalues of which are denoted
\sigma_{i}=\sigma_{i}({\mathbf{Y}})
, we have

\displaystyle{\rm Tr}\big({\mathbf{Y}}^{k}(\boldsymbol{\Omega}(t)-{\mathbf{J}}(t))^{-1}\big)=\sum_{i\leq N}\frac{\sigma_{i}^{k}}{\bar{V}(t)+\bar{\chi}(t)^{-1}-\sum_{\ell\leq K}c_{\ell}(t)\sigma_{i}^{\ell}}.

(281)

Then

\displaystyle\partial_{c_{k}}\Phi_{N}|_{t,{\mathbf{c}}(t)}

\displaystyle={\mathbf{m}}(t)^{\intercal}{\mathbf{Y}}^{k}\Big(\frac{1}{2}{\mathbf{m}}(t)-\eta({\mathbf{J}}(t),{\mathbf{m}}(t),\bar{V}(t))\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\leq N}\frac{\sigma_{i}^{k}}{\bar{V}(t)+\bar{\chi}(t)^{-1}-\sum_{\ell\leq K}c_{\ell}(t)\sigma_{i}^{\ell}}.

(282)

We aim at maximizing the free entropy so given a learning rate
\zeta>0
the learning rule finally reads

\displaystyle c_{k}(t+1)=c_{k}(t)+\zeta\partial_{c_{k}}\Phi_{N}|_{t,{\mathbf{c}}(t)}.

(283)

Appendix C
Proofs for BAMP

C.1
Auxiliary AMP

The iterates of the auxiliary AMP are denoted by
\tilde{{\mathbf{z}}}^{t},\tilde{{\mathbf{u}}}^{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
, and they are computed as follows, for
t\geq 1
:

\tilde{{\mathbf{z}}}^{t}={\mathbf{Z}}\tilde{{\mathbf{u}}}^{t}-\sum_{i=1}^{t}\bar{\sf b}_{t,i}\tilde{{\mathbf{u}}}^{i},\quad\tilde{{\mathbf{u}}}^{t+1}=\tilde{h}_{t+1}(\tilde{{\mathbf{z}}}^{1},\ldots,\tilde{{\mathbf{z}}}^{t},{\mathbf{u}}^{1},{\mathbf{X}}^{*}).

(284)

The iteration (
284
) is initialized with
\tilde{{\mathbf{u}}}^{1}={\mathbf{u}}^{1}
, where
{\mathbf{u}}^{1}
satisfies (
251
). For
t\geq 1
, the functions
\tilde{h}_{t+1}:\mathbb{R}^{t+2}\to\mathbb{R}
are applied component-wise, and they are recursively defined as

\begin{split}\tilde{h}_{K(t-1)+1+\ell}(&z_{1},\ldots,z_{K(t-1)+\ell},u_{1},x^{*})=z_{K(t-1)+\ell}+(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{K(t-1)+\ell})_{K(t-1)+\ell,1}\,u_{1}\\
&\hskip-40.00006pt+\hskip-5.0pt\sum_{i=2}^{K(t-1)+\ell}\hskip-5.0pt(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{K(t-1)+\ell})_{K(t-1)+\ell,i}\,\tilde{h}_{i}\Big(z_{1},\ldots,z_{i-1},u_{1},x^{*}\Big)+\tilde{\mu}_{K(t-1)+\ell}x^{*},\quad\ell\in[K-1],\\
\tilde{h}_{Kt+1}(&z_{1},\ldots,z_{Kt},u_{1},x^{*})=g_{t+1}\Big(\mu_{t}x^{*}+\sum_{i=1}^{Kt}\theta_{t,i}z_{i}\Big).\end{split}

(285)

The idea is that the choice (
285
) for the denoisers
\{\tilde{h}_{t+1}\}_{t\geq 1}
ensures that
\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{K(t-1)+\ell}
tracks the quantity
{\mathbf{Y}}^{\ell-1}{\mathbf{u}}^{t}
for
\ell\in[K]
and
t\geq 1
, where
\{{\mathbf{u}}^{t}\}
are the iterates of the AMP iteration (
249
) we are interested in analyzing.

In (
285
),
g_{t+1}
is the denoiser of the AMP (
249
). The parameters
(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{K(t-1)+\ell}
,
\tilde{\mu}_{K(t-1)+\ell}
,
\mu_{t},\theta_{t,i})
come from the state evolution recursion detailed in Section
10.2
:
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{K(t-1)+\ell}
is given by (
260
),
\tilde{\mu}_{K(t-1)+\ell}
by (
257
),
\mu_{t}
by (
265
) and
\theta_{t,i}
by (
266
). We now discuss how to obtain the coefficients
\{\bar{\sf b}_{t,i}\}_{i=1}^{t}
needed in (
284
). Let us define the matrix
\bar{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{t\times t}
as

(\bar{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{t})_{i,j}=0,\quad\mbox{ for }i\leq j,\qquad(\bar{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{t})_{i,j}=\langle\partial_{j}\tilde{{\mathbf{u}}}^{i}\rangle,\quad\mbox{ for }i>j,

(286)

where, for
j<i
, the vector
\langle\partial_{j}\tilde{{\mathbf{u}}}^{i}\rangle\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
denotes the partial derivative of
\tilde{h}_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{i+1}\to\mathbb{R}
with respect to the
j
-th input (applied component-wise). Then, the vector
(\bar{\sf b}_{t,1},\ldots,\bar{\sf b}_{t,t})
is given by the last row of the matrix
\bar{{\mathbf{B}}}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{t\times t}
defined as

\bar{{\mathbf{B}}}_{t}=\sum_{j=0}^{t-1}\kappa_{j+1}\bar{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{t}^{j}.

(287)

where
\{\kappa_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
denotes the sequence of free cumulants associated to the matrix
{\mathbf{Z}}
.

C.2
State evolution of auxiliary AMP

Using Theorem 2.3 in
[
110
]
, we provide a state evolution result for the auxiliary AMP (
284
). In particular, we show in Proposition
3
that the joint empirical distribution of
(\tilde{{\mathbf{z}}}^{1},\ldots,\tilde{{\mathbf{z}}}^{t})
converges to a
t
-dimensional Gaussian
\mathcal{N}({\mathbf{0}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{t})
.

The covariance matrices
\{\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{t}\}_{t\geq 1}
are defined recursively, starting with
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{1}=\bar{\kappa}_{2}\mathbb{E}[U_{1}^{2}]
, where
U_{1}
is defined in (
251
). Given
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{t}
, let

\begin{split}(\hat{Z}_{1}&,\ldots,\hat{Z}_{t})\sim\mathcal{N}({\mathbf{0}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{t})\mbox{ and independent of }(X^{*},U_{1}),\\
\hat{U}_{s}&=\tilde{h}_{s}\Big(\hat{Z}_{1},\ldots,\hat{Z}_{s-1},U_{1},X^{*}\Big),\quad\mbox{ for }s\in\{2,\ldots,t+1\},\end{split}

(288)

where
\tilde{h}_{s}
is defined via (
285
) and we set
\hat{U}_{1}=U_{1}
. Let
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{t+1},\hat{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{t+1}\in\mathbb{R}^{(t+1)\times(t+1)}
be matrices with entries given by

\begin{split}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{t+1})_{i,j}&=0,\quad\mbox{ for }i\leq j,\qquad\qquad(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{t+1})_{i,j}=\mathbb{E}[\partial_{j}\hat{U}_{i}],\quad\mbox{ for }i>j,\\
(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{t+1})_{i,j}&=\mathbb{E}[\hat{U}_{i}\,\hat{U}_{j}],\quad 1\leq i,j\leq t+1,\end{split}

(289)

where
\partial_{j}\hat{U}_{i}
denotes the partial derivative
\partial_{\hat{Z}_{j}}\tilde{h}_{i}(\hat{Z}_{1},\ldots,\hat{Z}_{i-1},U_{1},X)
. Then, we compute the covariance matrix
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{t+1}
as

\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{t+1}=\sum_{j=0}^{2t}\bar{\kappa}_{j+2}\sum_{i=0}^{j}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{t+1})^{i}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{t+1}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{t+1}^{\intercal})^{j-i}.

(290)

It can be verified that the
t\times t
top left sub-matrix of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{t+1}
is given by
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{t}
.

Proposition 3

(State evolution for auxiliary AMP)
.

Consider the auxiliary AMP in (
284
) and the state evolution random variables defined in (
288
). Let
\tilde{\psi}:\mathbb{R}^{2t+2}\to\mathbb{R}
be a
\mathrm{PL}(2)
function. Then, for each
t\geq 1
, we almost surely have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tilde{\psi}(\tilde{z}^{1}_{i},\ldots,\tilde{z}^{t}_{i},\tilde{u}^{1}_{i},\ldots,\tilde{u}^{t+1}_{i},X^{*}_{i})

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt=\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\psi}(\hat{Z}_{1},\ldots,\hat{Z}_{t},\hat{U}_{1},\ldots,\hat{U}_{t+1},X^{*})].

(291)

Equivalently, as
N\to\infty
, almost surely:

\displaystyle(\tilde{{\mathbf{z}}}^{1},\ldots,\tilde{{\mathbf{z}}}^{t},\,\tilde{{\mathbf{u}}}^{1},\ldots,\tilde{{\mathbf{u}}}^{t+1},\,{\mathbf{X}}^{*})\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{W_{2}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\,(\hat{Z}_{1},\ldots,\hat{Z}_{t},\hat{U}_{1},\ldots,\hat{U}_{t+1},X^{*}).

(292)

Furthermore,

(\hat{Z}_{1},\ldots,\hat{Z}_{t},\hat{U}_{1},\ldots,\hat{U}_{t+1},X^{*})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm d}}{{=}}(\tilde{Z}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{Z}_{t},\tilde{U}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{U}_{t+1},X^{*}),

(293)

where
(\tilde{Z}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{Z}_{t},\tilde{U}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{U}_{t+1},X^{*})
are obtained via (
254
)–(
256
).

Proof.

The result follows from Theorem 2.3 in
[
110
]
. In fact, Assumption 2.1 of
[
110
]
holds because of the model assumptions on
{\mathbf{Z}}
, Assumption 2.2(a) holds because
({\mathbf{X}}^{*},\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{1})=({\mathbf{X}}^{*},{\mathbf{u}}^{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{W_{2}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}(X^{*},U_{1})
from (
251
), and Assumption 2.2(b) follows from the definition of
\tilde{h}_{t+1}
in (
285
) and the fact that
g_{t+1}
is continuously differentiable and Lipschitz. As the auxiliary AMP in (
284
) is of the standard form for which the state evolution result of Theorem 2.3 in
[
110
]
holds, we readily obtain (
292
). The equivalence between (
292
) and (
291
) follows from
[
48
, Corollary 7.21]
. Finally, by inspecting the state evolution recursions (
254
)–(
256
) and (
288
) giving
(\tilde{Z}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{Z}_{t},\tilde{U}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{U}_{t+1},X^{*})
and
(\hat{Z}_{1},\ldots,\hat{Z}_{t},\hat{U}_{1},\ldots,\hat{U}_{t+1},X^{*})
respectively, (
293
) is readily obtained.
∎

Proposition
3
gives that the state evolution recursion discussed in Section
10.2
(cf. (
254
)–(
256
)) coincides with the state evolution tracking the iterates of the auxiliary AMP algorithm (
284
). In particular,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{3t}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{3t}
,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{3t}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{3t}
, and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{3t}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{3t}
. Furthermore, in the proof of Theorem
2
contained in Appendix
C.3
, we will show that
\bar{\mathbf{B}}_{3t}\to\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{3t}
as
N\to\infty
.

C.3
Proof of Theorem
2

We start by presenting a useful technical lemma.

Lemma 4
.

Let
F:\mathbb{R}^{t}\to\mathbb{R}
be a Lipschitz function, and let
\partial_{k}F
denote its derivative with respect to the
k
-th argument, for
1\leq k\leq t
. Assume that
\partial_{k}F
is continuous almost everywhere in the
k
-th argument, for each
k
. Let
(V_{1}^{(m)},\ldots,V_{t}^{(m)})
be a sequence of random vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{t}
converging in distribution to the random vector
(V_{1},\ldots,V_{t})
as
m\to\infty
. Furthermore, assume that the distribution of
(V_{1},\ldots,V_{t})
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure. Then,

\lim_{m\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[\partial_{k}F(V_{1}^{(m)},\ldots,V_{t}^{(m)})]=\mathbb{E}[\partial_{k}F(V_{1},\ldots,V_{t})],\qquad 1\leq k\leq t.

(294)

The result was proved for
t=2
in
[
24
, Lemma 6]
. The proof for
t>2
is basically the same, see also
[
48
, Lemma 7.14]
.
At this point, we are ready to give the proof of Theorem
2
.

Proof of Theorem
2
.

We show that, for any
\mathrm{PL}(2)
function
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{2t+2}\to\mathbb{R}
, the following limit holds almost surely for
t\geq 1
:

\begin{split}\lim_{N\to\infty}\Big|\frac{1}{N}&\sum_{i=1}^{N}\psi\big(u^{1}_{i},u^{2}_{i},\ldots,u^{t+1}_{i},f^{1}_{i},f^{2}_{i},\ldots,f^{t}_{i},X^{*}_{i}\big)\\
&-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\psi\big(\tilde{u}^{1}_{i},\tilde{u}^{K+1}_{i},\ldots,\tilde{u}^{Kt+1}_{i},\tilde{f}^{1}_{i},\tilde{f}^{2}_{i},\ldots,\tilde{f}^{t}_{i},X^{*}_{i}\big)\Big|=0,\end{split}

(295)

where we have defined for
s\in\{1,\ldots,t\}
,

\tilde{\mathbf{f}}^{s}=\mu_{s}{\mathbf{X}}^{*}+\sum_{i=1}^{Ks}\theta_{s,i}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}^{i}.

(296)

From here till the end of the argument, all the limits hold almost surely, and we use
C
to denote a generic positive constant, which can change from line to line and is independent of
N
. By using that
\psi
is pseudo-Lipschitz, we have that

\begin{split}\Big|&\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\psi\big(u^{1}_{i},u^{2}_{i},\ldots,u^{t+1}_{i},f^{1}_{i},f^{2}_{i},\ldots,f^{t}_{i},X^{*}_{i}\big)\\
&\hskip 30.00005pt-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\psi\big(\tilde{u}^{1}_{i},\tilde{u}^{K+1}_{i},\ldots,\tilde{u}^{Kt+1}_{i},\tilde{f}^{1}_{i},\tilde{f}^{2}_{i},\ldots,\tilde{f}^{t}_{i},X^{*}_{i}\big)\Big|\\
&\leq\frac{C}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Big(1+|X^{*}_{i}|+2|u^{1}_{i}|+\sum_{k=1}^{t}\Big(|f^{k}_{i}|+|\tilde{f}^{k}_{i}|+|u^{k+1}_{i}|+|\tilde{u}^{Kk+1}_{i}|\Big)\Big)\\
&\hskip 140.00021pt\cdot\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{t}\big(|f^{k}_{i}-\tilde{f}^{k}_{i}|^{2}+|u^{k+1}_{i}-\tilde{u}^{Kk+1}_{i}|^{2}\big)\Big)^{1/2}\\
&\leq C(4t+3)\Big[1+\frac{\|{\mathbf{X}}^{*}\|^{2}}{N}+\sum_{k=1}^{t}\Big(\frac{\|{\mathbf{f}}^{k}\|^{2}}{N}+\frac{\|\tilde{\mathbf{f}}^{k}\|^{2}}{N}+\frac{\|{\mathbf{u}}^{k+1}\|^{2}}{N}+\frac{\|\tilde{{\mathbf{u}}}^{Kk+1}\|^{2}}{N}\Big)\Big]^{1/2}\\
&\hskip 130.0002pt\cdot\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{t}\Big(\frac{\|{\mathbf{f}}^{k}-\tilde{\mathbf{f}}^{k}\|^{2}}{N}+\frac{\|{\mathbf{u}}^{k+1}-\tilde{{\mathbf{u}}}^{Kk+1}\|^{2}}{N}\,\Big)\Big)^{1/2},\end{split}

(297)

where the last step uses twice Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. We now inductively show that as
N\to\infty
:
(i)
each of the terms in the last line of (
297
) converges to zero, and
(ii)
the terms within the square brackets in (
297
) all converge to finite, deterministic limits. To achieve this goal, we will also show that, for
k\in[t]
and
\ell\in[K-1]
,

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}

\displaystyle\frac{\|{\mathbf{Y}}^{\ell}{\mathbf{u}}^{k}-\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{K(k-1)+1+\ell}\|^{2}}{N}=0,

(298)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}

\displaystyle\frac{\|\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{K(k-1)+1+\ell}\hskip-1.99997pt-\hskip-1.99997pt\sum_{j=1}^{K(k-1)+\ell}\hskip-1.99997pt\alpha_{K(k-1)+1+\ell,j}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}^{j}\hskip-1.99997pt-\hskip-1.99997pt\sum_{j=1}^{k}\hskip-1.99997pt\beta_{K(k-1)+1+\ell,j}{\mathbf{u}}^{j}\hskip-1.99997pt-\hskip-1.99997pt\gamma_{K(k-1)+1+\ell}{\mathbf{X}}^{*}\|^{2}}{N}

\displaystyle\hskip 325.00049pt=0.

(299)

The limit (
298
) formalizes the idea discussed in Section
10.1
(see (
250
)) that the iterate
\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{K(k-1)+1+\ell}
of the auxiliary AMP tracks the quantity
{\mathbf{Y}}^{\ell}{\mathbf{u}}^{k}
, where
{\mathbf{u}}^{k}
is the iterate of the AMP we wish to analyze, up to an
o_{N}(1)
error. The limit (
299
) formalizes the interpretation of the coefficients
\{\alpha_{i,j}\}
,
\{\beta_{i,j}\}
,
\{\gamma_{i}\}
provided at the end of Section
10.2
(see (
271
)).

Base case (
t=1
).
We have that

\begin{split}{\mathbf{Y}}{\mathbf{u}}^{1}-\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{2}&={\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{u}}^{1}+\lambda\frac{\langle{\mathbf{X}}^{*},{\mathbf{u}}^{1}\rangle}{N}{\mathbf{X}}^{*}-\tilde{{\mathbf{z}}}^{1}-(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{1})_{1,1}{\mathbf{u}}^{1}-\tilde{\mu}_{1}{\mathbf{X}}^{*}\\
&=\Big(\lambda\frac{\langle{\mathbf{X}}^{*},{\mathbf{u}}^{1}\rangle}{N}-\tilde{\mu}_{1}\Big){\mathbf{X}}^{*}+\big(\bar{\sf b}_{1,1}-(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{1})_{1,1}\big){\mathbf{u}}^{1},\end{split}

(300)

where the first equality uses the definition of
{\mathbf{Y}}
and of
\tilde{h}_{2}
(see (
285
)), and the second equality uses (
284
) and that
\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{1}={\mathbf{u}}^{1}
. Hence, by triangle inequality,

\begin{split}\frac{\|{\mathbf{Y}}{\mathbf{u}}^{1}-\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{2}\|^{2}}{N}&\leq 2\Big(\lambda\frac{\langle{\mathbf{X}}^{*},{\mathbf{u}}^{1}\rangle}{N}-\tilde{\mu}_{1}\Big)^{2}\frac{\|{\mathbf{X}}^{*}\|^{2}}{N}+2\big(\bar{\sf b}_{1,1}-(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{1})_{1,1}\big)^{2}\frac{\|{\mathbf{u}}^{1}\|^{2}}{N}\\
&\leq C\Big(\Big(\lambda\frac{\langle{\mathbf{X}}^{*},{\mathbf{u}}^{1}\rangle}{N}-\tilde{\mu}_{1}\Big)^{2}+\big(\bar{\sf b}_{1,1}-(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{1})_{1,1}\big)^{2}\Big),\end{split}

(301)

where the last inequality uses that
({\mathbf{X}}^{*},{\mathbf{u}}^{1})
converges in
W_{2}
to a pair of random variables with finite second moments. As
\tilde{\mu}_{1}=\lambda\epsilon
(cf. (
253
)), we have

\lim_{N\to\infty}\lambda\frac{\langle{\mathbf{X}}^{*},{\mathbf{u}}^{1}\rangle}{N}=\lambda\mathbb{E}[U_{1}X^{*}]=\lambda\epsilon=\tilde{\mu}_{1}.

(302)

Furthermore, note that
(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{1})_{1,1}=\bar{\kappa}_{1}
(cf. (
253
)) and
\bar{\sf b}_{1,1}=\kappa_{1}
(cf. (
287
)). Hence, by the model assumptions, as
N\to\infty
,
\kappa_{1}\to\bar{\kappa}_{1}
and, therefore,
\bar{\sf b}_{1,1}\to(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{1})_{1,1}
. By combining this observation with (
301
) and (
302
), we obtain that (
298
) holds for
k=1
and
\ell=1
.

By using (
262
)–(
264
), we readily obtain that
\alpha_{2,1}=1
,
\beta_{2,1}=(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{1})_{1,1}
and
\gamma_{2}=\tilde{\mu}_{1}
. Hence, by using the definition (
285
) of
\tilde{h}_{2}
, we obtain that (
299
) holds for
k=1
and
\ell=1
.

Next, by using the definitions of
{\mathbf{Y}}
, of the auxiliary AMP (
284
) and of
\tilde{h}_{3}
(cf. (
285
)), we have

\begin{split}&{\mathbf{Y}}^{2}{\mathbf{u}}^{1}-\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{3}={\mathbf{Y}}({\mathbf{Y}}{\mathbf{u}}^{1}-\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{2})+{\mathbf{Y}}\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{2}-\tilde{\mathbf{z}}^{2}-(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{2})_{2,1}{\mathbf{u}}^{1}-(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{2})_{2,2}\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{2}-\tilde{\mu}_{2}{\mathbf{X}}^{*}\\
&={\mathbf{Y}}({\mathbf{Y}}{\mathbf{u}}^{1}-\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{2})+{\mathbf{Z}}\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{2}-\tilde{\mathbf{z}}^{2}-(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{2})_{2,1}{\mathbf{u}}^{1}-(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{2})_{2,2}\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{2}+\Big(\lambda\frac{\langle{\mathbf{X}}^{*},\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{2}\rangle}{N}-\tilde{\mu}_{2}\Big){\mathbf{X}}^{*}\\
&={\mathbf{Y}}({\mathbf{Y}}{\mathbf{u}}^{1}-\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{2})+\big(\bar{\sf b}_{2,1}-(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{2})_{2,1}\big){\mathbf{u}}^{1}+\big(\bar{\sf b}_{2,2}-(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{2})_{2,2}\big)\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{2}+\Big(\lambda\frac{\langle{\mathbf{X}}^{*},\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{2}\rangle}{N}-\tilde{\mu}_{2}\Big){\mathbf{X}}^{*}.\end{split}

(303)

Hence, by triangle inequality,

\begin{split}\frac{\|{\mathbf{Y}}^{2}{\mathbf{u}}^{1}-\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{3}\|^{2}}{N}&\leq C\Big(\frac{\|{\mathbf{Y}}({\mathbf{Y}}{\mathbf{u}}^{1}-\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{2})\|^{2}}{N}+\big(\bar{\sf b}_{2,1}-(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{2})_{2,1}\big)^{2}\frac{\|{\mathbf{u}}^{1}\|^{2}}{N}\\
&+\big(\bar{\sf b}_{2,2}-(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{2})_{2,2}\big)^{2}\frac{\|\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{2}\|^{2}}{N}+\Big(\lambda\frac{\langle{\mathbf{X}}^{*},\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{2}\rangle}{N}-\tilde{\mu}_{2}\Big)^{2}\frac{\|{\mathbf{X}}^{*}\|^{2}}{N}\Big)\\
&:=C(T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3}+T_{4}).\end{split}

(304)

Consider the first term. As
{\mathbf{Y}}
has bounded operator norm and (
298
) holds for
k=1
and
\ell=1
, we have that
T_{1}\to 0
as
N\to\infty
.

Consider the second and third terms. The following chain of equalities holds

\lim_{N\to\infty}(\bar{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{2})_{2,1}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\langle\partial_{1}\tilde{{\mathbf{u}}}^{2}\rangle=\mathbb{E}[\partial_{1}\hat{U}_{2}]=\mathbb{E}[\partial_{1}\tilde{U}_{2}]=(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{2})_{2,1}.

(305)

Here, the first equality uses the definition (
286
); the second equality follows from Lemma
4
, as
\tilde{{\mathbf{u}}}^{2}
converges in
W_{2}
(and therefore in distribution) to
\tilde{U}_{2}
and
\partial_{1}\tilde{U}_{2}
is continuous; the third equality uses (
293
); and the fourth equality uses the definition of
(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{2})_{2,1}
in (
259
). By the model assumptions, as
N\to\infty
,
\kappa_{j}\to\bar{\kappa}_{j}
for all
j
. Thus, by combining (
305
) with the definitions of
\bar{\mathbf{B}}_{2}
and
\tilde{{\mathbf{B}}}_{2}
in (
287
) and (
260
), respectively, we conclude that, as
N\to\infty
,
\bar{\sf b}_{2,i}\to(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{2})_{2,i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
. By Proposition
3
,
\|\tilde{{\mathbf{u}}}^{2}\|^{2}/N
converges to a finite limit, hence we conclude that
T_{2},T_{3}\to 0
as
N\to\infty
.

Consider the fourth term. Then,

\lim_{N\to\infty}\lambda\frac{\langle{\mathbf{X}}^{*},\tilde{{\mathbf{u}}}^{2}\rangle}{N}=\lambda\mathbb{E}[X^{*}\,\tilde{U}_{2}]=\tilde{\mu}_{2}.

Here, the first equality uses Proposition
3
and the second equality uses the definition of
\tilde{\mu}_{2}
in (
257
). As
\|{\mathbf{X}}^{*}\|^{2}/N=1
, we conclude that
T_{4}\to 0
as
N\to\infty
. This proves that the RHS of (
304
) vanishes and gives that (
298
) holds for
k=1
and
\ell=2
.

By using (
262
)–(
264
), we readily obtain that
\alpha_{3,1}=(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{2})_{2,2}
,
\alpha_{3,2}=1
,
\beta_{3,1}=(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{2})_{2,1}+(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{2})_{2,2}\,(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{2})_{1,1}
and
\gamma_{3}=\tilde{\mu}_{2}+\tilde{\mu}_{1}\,(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{2})_{2,2}
. Hence, by using the definition (
285
) of
\tilde{h}_{3}
, we obtain that (
299
) holds for
k=1
and
\ell=2
.

The proof of (
298
)–(
299
) for
k=1
and
\ell\in\{3,\ldots,K-1\}
follows from similar arguments. In particular, we write

\begin{split}&{\mathbf{Y}}^{\ell}{\mathbf{u}}^{1}-\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{1+\ell}={\mathbf{Y}}({\mathbf{Y}}^{\ell-1}{\mathbf{u}}^{1}-\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{\ell})+{\mathbf{Y}}\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{\ell}-\tilde{\mathbf{z}}^{\ell}-(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\ell})_{\ell,1}{\mathbf{u}}^{1}-\sum_{j=2}^{\ell}(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\ell})_{\ell,j}\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{j}-\tilde{\mu}_{\ell}{\mathbf{X}}^{*}\\
&={\mathbf{Y}}({\mathbf{Y}}^{\ell-1}{\mathbf{u}}^{1}-\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{\ell})+{\mathbf{Z}}\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{\ell}-\tilde{\mathbf{z}}^{\ell}-(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\ell})_{\ell,1}{\mathbf{u}}^{1}-\sum_{j=2}^{\ell}(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\ell})_{\ell,j}\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{j}+\Big(\lambda\frac{\langle{\mathbf{X}}^{*},\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{\ell}\rangle}{N}-\tilde{\mu}_{\ell}\Big){\mathbf{X}}^{*}\\
&={\mathbf{Y}}({\mathbf{Y}}^{\ell-1}{\mathbf{u}}^{1}-\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{\ell})+\big(\bar{\sf b}_{\ell,1}-(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\ell})_{\ell,1}\big){\mathbf{u}}^{1}+\sum_{j=2}^{\ell}\big(\bar{\sf b}_{\ell,j}-(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\ell})_{\ell,j}\big)\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{j}\\
&\hskip 200.0003pt+\Big(\lambda\frac{\langle{\mathbf{X}}^{*},\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{\ell}\rangle}{N}-\tilde{\mu}_{\ell}\Big){\mathbf{X}}^{*},\end{split}

(306)

which by triangle inequality gives

\begin{split}\frac{\|{\mathbf{Y}}^{\ell}{\mathbf{u}}^{1}-\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{1+\ell}\|^{2}}{N}&\leq C\Big(\frac{\|{\mathbf{Y}}({\mathbf{Y}}^{\ell-1}{\mathbf{u}}^{1}-\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{\ell})\|^{2}}{N}+\big(\bar{\sf b}_{\ell,1}-(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\ell})_{\ell,1}\big)^{2}\frac{\|{\mathbf{u}}^{1}\|^{2}}{N}\\
&+\sum_{j=2}^{\ell}\big(\bar{\sf b}_{\ell,j}-(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\ell})_{\ell,j}\big)^{2}\frac{\|\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{j}\|^{2}}{N}+\Big(\lambda\frac{\langle{\mathbf{X}}^{*},\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{\ell}\rangle}{N}-\tilde{\mu}_{\ell}\Big)^{2}\frac{\|{\mathbf{X}}^{*}\|^{2}}{N}\Big).\end{split}

(307)

As
{\mathbf{Y}}
has bounded operator norm and
\|{\mathbf{Y}}^{\ell-1}{\mathbf{u}}^{1}-\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{\ell}\|^{2}/N\to 0
(by the previous step), we have that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\|{\mathbf{Y}}({\mathbf{Y}}^{\ell-1}{\mathbf{u}}^{1}-\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{\ell})\|^{2}}{N}=0.

Next, by following passages analogous to those in (
305
), we have that
\lim_{N\to\infty}\bar{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\ell}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\ell}
. As
\kappa_{j}\to\bar{\kappa}_{j}
for all
j
, this implies that
\lim_{N\to\infty}\bar{\mathbf{B}}_{\ell}=\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\ell}
. Hence, for all
j\in[\ell]
, as
\|\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{j}\|/N
is bounded, we have that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\Bigg(\big(\bar{\sf b}_{\ell,1}-(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\ell})_{\ell,1}\big)^{2}\frac{\|{\mathbf{u}}^{1}\|^{2}}{N}+\sum_{j=2}^{\ell}\big(\bar{\sf b}_{\ell,j}-(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\ell})_{\ell,j}\big)^{2}\frac{\|\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{j}\|^{2}}{N}\Bigg)=0.

Finally, as

\lim_{N\to\infty}\lambda\frac{\langle{\mathbf{X}}^{*},\tilde{{\mathbf{u}}}^{\ell}\rangle}{N}=\lambda\mathbb{E}[X^{*}\,\tilde{U}_{\ell}]=\tilde{\mu}_{\ell},

we conclude that the last term in the RHS of (
307
) vanishes as well, which proves that (
298
) holds for
k=1
and a generic
\ell\in\{3,\ldots,K-1\}
. Furthermore, by using (
262
)–(
264
) and the definition (
285
) of
\tilde{h}_{\ell+1}
, one can readily verify that (
299
) holds for
k=1
and a generic
\ell\in\{3,\ldots,K-1\}
.

By using (
284
) and the definition of
{\mathbf{Y}}
, we have that

\begin{split}{\mathbf{Y}}^{K}{\mathbf{u}}^{1}-\tilde{\mathbf{z}}^{K}-\sum_{i=1}^{K}\bar{\sf b}_{K,i}\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{i}-\tilde{\mu}_{K}{\mathbf{X}}^{*}&={\mathbf{Z}}\big({\mathbf{Y}}^{K-1}{\mathbf{u}}^{1}-\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{K}\big)+\Big(\lambda\frac{\langle{\mathbf{X}}^{*},{\mathbf{Y}}^{K-1}{\mathbf{u}}^{1}\rangle}{N}-\tilde{\mu}_{K}\Big){\mathbf{X}}^{*}.\end{split}

(308)

Hence, by using the definition of
\tilde{\mu}_{K}
in (
257
) and (
298
) with
k=1
,
\ell=K-1
, we obtain

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\|{\mathbf{Y}}^{K}{\mathbf{u}}^{1}-\tilde{\mathbf{z}}^{K}-\sum_{i=1}^{K}\bar{\sf b}_{K,i}\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{i}-\tilde{\mu}_{K}{\mathbf{X}}^{*}\|^{2}}{N}=0.

(309)

Recall that
{\mathbf{J}}({\mathbf{Y}})=\sum_{j=1}^{K}c_{j}{\mathbf{Y}}^{j}
. Then, by combining (
298
) with
k=1
and (
309
), we have

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\|{\mathbf{J}}({\mathbf{Y}}){\mathbf{u}}^{1}-\sum_{j=1}^{K}c_{j}\big(\tilde{\mathbf{z}}^{j}+\sum_{i=1}^{j}\bar{\sf b}_{j,i}\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{i}+\tilde{\mu}_{j}{\mathbf{X}}^{*}\big)\|^{2}}{N}=0.

(310)

By following the same argument as in (
305
), we have that
\lim_{N\to\infty}\bar{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{K}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{K}
. As
\kappa_{j}\to\bar{\kappa}_{j}
for all
j
, this implies that
\lim_{N\to\infty}\bar{\mathbf{B}}_{K}=\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{K}
. Therefore,

\begin{split}\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\bigg\|\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{K}c_{j}\big(\tilde{\mathbf{z}}^{j}+\sum_{i=1}^{j}\bar{\sf b}_{j,i}\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{i}+\tilde{\mu}_{j}{\mathbf{X}}^{*}\big)-\sum_{j=1}^{K}c_{j}\big(\tilde{\mathbf{z}}^{j}+\sum_{i=1}^{j}(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{j})_{j,i}\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{i}+\tilde{\mu}_{j}{\mathbf{X}}^{*}\big)\bigg\|^{2}}{N}=0.\end{split}

(311)

Recall that
\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{1}={\mathbf{u}}^{1}
and (
299
) holds for
k=1
. Hence, by plugging in the formulas for
{\sf c}_{1,1}
,
\mu_{1}
and
\{\theta_{1,i}\}_{i\in[K]}
(cf. (
267
), (
265
) and (
266
)), we have

\begin{split}\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\bigg\|\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{K}c_{j}\big(\tilde{\mathbf{z}}^{j}+\sum_{i=1}^{j}(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{j})_{j,i}\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{i}+\tilde{\mu}_{j}{\mathbf{X}}^{*}\big)-{\sf c}_{1,1}{\mathbf{u}}^{1}-\mu_{1}{\mathbf{X}}^{*}-\sum_{i=1}^{K}\theta_{1,i}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}^{i}\bigg\|^{2}}{N}=0.\end{split}

(312)

By combining (
310
)–(
312
) with the definitions of
{\mathbf{f}}^{1}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{f}}^{1}
(cf. (
249
) and (
296
)), we conclude that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\|{\mathbf{f}}^{1}-\tilde{\mathbf{f}}^{1}\|^{2}}{N}=0.

(313)

As
g_{2}
is Lipschitz, (
313
) immediately implies that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\|{\mathbf{u}}^{2}-\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{K+1}\|^{2}}{N}=0.

(314)

An application of the triangle inequality gives that, for any
i\geq 1
,

\begin{split}\|\tilde{\mathbf{f}}^{i}\|-\|{\mathbf{f}}^{i}-\tilde{\mathbf{f}}^{i}\|&\leq\|{\mathbf{f}}^{i}\|\leq\|\tilde{\mathbf{f}}^{i}\|+\|{\mathbf{f}}^{i}-\tilde{\mathbf{f}}^{i}\|,\\
\|\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{Ki+1}\|-\|{\mathbf{u}}^{i+1}-\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{Ki+1}\|&\leq\|{\mathbf{u}}^{i+1}\|\leq\|\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{Ki+1}\|+\|{\mathbf{u}}^{i+1}-\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{Ki+1}\|.\end{split}

(315)

Thus, by using (
315
) with
i=1
and Proposition
3
, we obtain that

\begin{split}&\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\|{\mathbf{f}}^{1}\|^{2}}{N}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\|\tilde{\mathbf{f}}^{1}\|^{2}}{N}=\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\mu_{1}X^{*}+\sum_{i=1}^{K}\theta_{1,i}\tilde{Z}_{i}\Big)^{2}\Big],\\
&\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\|{\mathbf{u}}^{2}\|^{2}}{N}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\|\tilde{{\mathbf{u}}}^{K+1}\|^{2}}{N}=\mathbb{E}[(\tilde{U}_{K+1})^{2}],\end{split}

(316)

which concludes the base step.

Induction step.
Assume towards induction that (
298
)–(
299
) hold for
k\in[t]
,
\ell\in[K-1]
and that, for
k\in[t]
,

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\|{\mathbf{f}}^{k}-\tilde{\mathbf{f}}^{k}\|^{2}}{N}=0,

(317)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\|{\mathbf{u}}^{k+1}-\tilde{{\mathbf{u}}}^{Kk+1}\|^{2}}{N}=0,

(318)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\|{\mathbf{f}}^{k}\|^{2}}{N}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\|\tilde{\mathbf{f}}^{k}\|^{2}}{N}=\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\mu_{k}X^{*}+\sum_{i=1}^{Kk}\theta_{k,i}\tilde{Z}_{i}\Big)^{2}\Big],

(319)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\|{\mathbf{u}}^{k+1}\|^{2}}{N}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\|\tilde{{\mathbf{u}}}^{Kk+1}\|^{2}}{N}=\mathbb{E}[\tilde{U}_{Kk+1}^{2}].

(320)

We now show that (
317
)–(
320
) hold for
k=t+1
, and that (
298
)–(
299
) hold for
k=t+1
,
\ell\in[K-1]
.
By doing so, we will have proved also the induction step and consequently that (
295
) holds.

Using similar passages as in (
300
), we obtain

\begin{split}&{\mathbf{Y}}{\mathbf{u}}^{t+1}-\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{Kt+2}={\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{u}}^{t+1}+\lambda\frac{\langle{\mathbf{X}}^{*},{\mathbf{u}}^{t+1}\rangle}{N}{\mathbf{X}}^{*}-\tilde{{\mathbf{z}}}^{Kt+1}-\sum_{i=1}^{Kt+1}(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{Kt+1})_{Kt+1,i}\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{i}-\tilde{\mu}_{Kt+1}{\mathbf{X}}^{*}\\
&={\mathbf{Z}}({\mathbf{u}}^{t+1}-\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{Kt+1})+\Big(\lambda\frac{\langle{\mathbf{X}}^{*},{\mathbf{u}}^{t+1}\rangle}{N}-\tilde{\mu}_{Kt+1}\Big){\mathbf{X}}^{*}+\sum_{i=1}^{Kt+1}\big(\bar{\sf b}_{Kt+1,i}-(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{Kt+1})_{Kt+1,i}\big)\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{i}.\end{split}

(321)

Hence, by triangle inequality,

\begin{split}&\frac{\|{\mathbf{Y}}{\mathbf{u}}^{t+1}-\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{Kt+2}\|^{2}}{N}\leq C\Big(\frac{\|{\mathbf{Z}}({\mathbf{u}}^{t+1}-\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{Kt+1})\|^{2}}{N}\\
&\hskip 10.00002pt+\Big(\lambda\frac{\langle{\mathbf{X}}^{*},{\mathbf{u}}^{t+1}\rangle}{N}-\tilde{\mu}_{Kt+1}\Big)^{2}\frac{\|{\mathbf{X}}^{*}\|^{2}}{N}+\sum_{i=1}^{Kt+1}\big(\bar{\sf b}_{Kt+1,i}-(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{Kt+1})_{Kt+1,i}\big)^{2}\frac{\|\tilde{{\mathbf{u}}}^{i}\|^{2}}{N}\Big)\\
&\hskip 20.00003pt:=C(\bar{T}_{1}+\bar{T}_{2}+\bar{T}_{3}).\end{split}

(322)

Consider the first term. Since
\|{\mathbf{Z}}\|_{\rm op}\leq C
, the induction hypothesis (
318
) implies that
\bar{T}_{1}\to 0
as
N\to\infty
.

Consider the second term. The following chain of equalities holds:

\lim_{N\to\infty}\lambda\frac{\langle{\mathbf{X}}^{*},{\mathbf{u}}^{t+1}\rangle}{N}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\lambda\frac{\langle{\mathbf{X}}^{*},\tilde{{\mathbf{u}}}^{Kt+1}\rangle}{N}=\lambda\mathbb{E}[X\,\tilde{U}_{Kt+1}]=\tilde{\mu}_{Kt+1}.

(323)

Here, the first equality uses (
318
) together with the fact that
\|{\mathbf{X}}^{*}\|^{2}/N=1
; the second equality follows from Proposition
3
; and the third equality uses the definition of
\tilde{\mu}_{Kt+1}
in (
257
). Finally, using (
323
) and again that
\|{\mathbf{X}}^{*}\|^{2}/N=1
gives that
\bar{T}_{2}\to 0
as
N\to\infty
.

Consider the third term. By following the same argument as in (
305
), we have that
\lim_{N\to\infty}\bar{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{Kt+1}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{Kt+1}
. As
\kappa_{j}\to\bar{\kappa}_{j}
for all
j
, this implies that
\lim_{N\to\infty}\bar{\mathbf{B}}_{Kt+1}=\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{Kt+1}
.
By using the induction hypothesis (
320
), which shows that
\|\tilde{{\mathbf{u}}}^{i}\|^{2}/N
converges to a finite limit, we conclude that
\bar{T}_{3}\to 0
as
N\to\infty
. This proves that the RHS of (
322
) vanishes and gives that (
298
) holds for
k=t+1
and
\ell=1
.

For
\ell\in\{2,\ldots,K-1\}
, by following passages similar to (
306
), we have

\begin{split}{\mathbf{Y}}^{\ell}{\mathbf{u}}^{t+1}-\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{Kt+\ell+1}&={\mathbf{Y}}({\mathbf{Y}}^{\ell-1}{\mathbf{u}}^{t+1}-\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{Kt+\ell})+\sum_{i=1}^{Kt+\ell}\big(\bar{\sf b}_{Kt+\ell,i}-(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{Kt+\ell})_{Kt+\ell,i}\big)\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{i}\\
&+\Big(\lambda\frac{\langle{\mathbf{X}}^{*},\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{Kt+\ell}\rangle}{N}-\tilde{\mu}_{Kt+\ell}\Big){\mathbf{X}}^{*},\end{split}

which by triangle inequality gives

\begin{split}&\frac{\|{\mathbf{Y}}^{\ell}{\mathbf{u}}^{t+1}-\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{Kt+\ell+1}\|^{2}}{N}\leq C\Big(\frac{\|{\mathbf{Y}}({\mathbf{Y}}^{\ell-1}{\mathbf{u}}^{t+1}-\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{Kt+\ell})\|^{2}}{N}\\
&\hskip 30.00005pt+\sum_{i=1}^{Kt+\ell}\big(\bar{\sf b}_{Kt+\ell,i}-(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{Kt+\ell})_{Kt+\ell,i}\big)^{2}\frac{\|\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{i}\|^{2}}{N}+\Big(\lambda\frac{\langle{\mathbf{X}}^{*},\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{Kt+\ell}\rangle}{N}-\tilde{\mu}_{Kt+\ell}\Big)^{2}\frac{\|{\mathbf{X}}^{*}\|^{2}}{N}\Big).\end{split}

(324)

The first term on the RHS of (
324
) vanishes as
{\mathbf{Y}}
has bounded operator norm and we have just proved in the previous step that
\|{\mathbf{Y}}^{\ell-1}{\mathbf{u}}^{t+1}-\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{Kt+\ell}\|^{2}/N\to 0
. To bound the second term, note that, by following the same argument as in (
305
), we have that
\lim_{N\to\infty}\bar{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{Kt+\ell}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{Kt+\ell}
. As
\kappa_{j}\to\bar{\kappa}_{j}
for all
j
, this implies that
\lim_{N\to\infty}\bar{\mathbf{B}}_{Kt+\ell}=\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{Kt+\ell}
.
By using the induction hypothesis (
320
), we have that
\|\tilde{{\mathbf{u}}}^{i}\|^{2}/N
converges to a finite limit for
i\in[Kt+\ell-1]
. Furthermore, as
\|{\mathbf{Y}}^{\ell-1}{\mathbf{u}}^{t+1}-\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{Kt+\ell}\|^{2}/N\to 0
, we also have that
\|\tilde{{\mathbf{u}}}^{Kt+\ell}\|^{2}/N
converges to a finite limit. As a result, the second term on the RHS of (
324
) vanishes. Finally, we can write a chain of equalities analogous to (
323
) with
Kt+\ell
in place of
Kt+1
, from which we deduce that the third term vanishes. This concludes the proof that (
298
) holds for
k=t+1
and
\ell\in[K-1]
.

For
\ell\in[K-1]
, by definition (
285
) of
h_{Kt+1+\ell}
, we have

\begin{split}\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{Kt+1+\ell}&=\tilde{\mathbf{z}}^{Kt+\ell}+\tilde{\mu}_{Kt+\ell}{\mathbf{X}}^{*}+\sum_{i=1}^{Kt+\ell}(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{Kt+\ell})_{Kt+\ell,i}\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{i}.\end{split}

(325)

Let us define:

\begin{split}\hat{\mathbf{u}}^{Kt+1+\ell}&:=\tilde{\mathbf{z}}^{Kt+\ell}+\tilde{\mu}_{Kt+\ell}{\mathbf{X}}^{*}+\sum_{i=1}^{t+1}(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{Kt+\ell})_{Kt+\ell,K(i-1)+1}{\mathbf{u}}^{i}\\
&\hskip 10.00002pt+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\not\equiv 1({\rm mod}K)\end{subarray}}^{Kt+\ell}(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{Kt+\ell})_{Kt+\ell,i}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}\alpha_{i,j}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}^{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{\lceil(i-1)/K\rceil}\beta_{i,j}{\mathbf{u}}^{j}+\gamma_{i}{\mathbf{X}}^{*}\Big).\end{split}

(326)

Then, by using the recursive definitions (
262
)–(
264
), we readily have that the RHS of (
326
) is equal to

\sum_{j=1}^{Kt+\ell}\alpha_{Kt+1+\ell,j}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}^{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{t+1}\beta_{Kt+1+\ell,j}{\mathbf{u}}^{j}+\gamma_{Kt+1+\ell}{\mathbf{X}}^{*}.

(327)

Recall that, by induction hypothesis, (
318
) holds for
k\in[t]
, and (
299
) holds for
k\in[t]
and
\ell\in[K-1]
. Thus, by using the expressions in (
325
) and (
326
) for
\ell=1
, one readily obtains that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\|\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{Kt+2}-\hat{\mathbf{u}}^{Kt+2}\|^{2}}{N}=0.

(328)

Since the RHS of (
326
) is equal to the expression in (
327
) for
\ell=1
, we conclude that (
299
) holds for
k=t+1
and
\ell=1
. At this point, we have that (
299
) holds for
k\in[t]
,
\ell\in[K-1]
and also for
k=t+1,\ell=1
. Hence, by using the expressions in (
325
) and (
326
) for
\ell=2
, we obtain

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\|\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{Kt+3}-\hat{\mathbf{u}}^{Kt+3}\|^{2}}{N}=0.

(329)

Since the RHS of (
326
) is equal to the expression in (
327
) for
\ell=2
, we conclude that (
299
) holds for
k=t+1
,
\ell=2
. By iterating this procedure for
\ell\in\{3,\ldots,K-1\}
, we obtain that (
299
) holds for
k=t+1
,
\ell\in[K-1]
.

By using (
284
) and the definition of
{\mathbf{Y}}
, we have that

\begin{split}{\mathbf{Y}}^{K}{\mathbf{u}}^{t+1}-\tilde{\mathbf{z}}^{K(t+1)}-&\sum_{i=1}^{K(t+1)}\bar{\sf b}_{K(t+1),i}\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{i}-\tilde{\mu}_{K(t+1)}{\mathbf{X}}^{*}\\
&={\mathbf{Z}}\big({\mathbf{Y}}^{K-1}{\mathbf{u}}^{t+1}-\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{K(t+1)}\big)+\Big(\lambda\frac{\langle{\mathbf{X}}^{*},{\mathbf{Y}}^{K-1}{\mathbf{u}}^{t+1}\rangle}{N}-\tilde{\mu}_{K(t+1)}\Big){\mathbf{X}}^{*}.\end{split}

(330)

Hence, by using (
298
) with
k=t+1
,
\ell=K-1
and the definition of
\tilde{\mu}_{K(t+1)}
in (
257
), we obtain

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\|{\mathbf{Y}}^{K}{\mathbf{u}}^{t+1}-\tilde{\mathbf{z}}^{K(t+1)}-\sum_{i=1}^{K(t+1)}\bar{\sf b}_{K(t+1),i}\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{i}-\tilde{\mu}_{K(t+1)}{\mathbf{X}}^{*}\|^{2}}{N}=0.

(331)

As
{\mathbf{J}}({\mathbf{Y}})=\sum_{j=1}^{K}c_{j}{\mathbf{Y}}^{j}
, by combining (
331
) with (
298
) with
k=t+1
,
\ell\in[K-1]
, we have

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\|{\mathbf{J}}({\mathbf{Y}}){\mathbf{u}}^{t+1}-\sum_{j=1}^{K}c_{j}\big(\tilde{\mathbf{z}}^{Kt+j}+\sum_{i=1}^{Kt+j}\bar{\sf b}_{Kt+j,i}\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{i}+\tilde{\mu}_{Kt+j}{\mathbf{X}}^{*}\big)\|^{2}}{N}=0.

(332)

By following the same argument as in (
305
), we have that
\lim_{N\to\infty}\bar{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{Kt+j}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{Kt+j}
for all
j\in[K]
. As
\kappa_{j}\to\bar{\kappa}_{j}
for all
j
, this implies that
\lim_{N\to\infty}\bar{\mathbf{B}}_{Kt+j}=\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{Kt+j}
for all
j\in[K]
. Therefore, (
332
) implies that

\begin{split}\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\|{\mathbf{J}}({\mathbf{Y}}){\mathbf{u}}^{t+1}-\sum_{j=1}^{K}c_{j}\big(\tilde{\mathbf{z}}^{Kt+j}+\sum_{i=1}^{Kt+j}(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{Kt+j})_{Kt+j,i}\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{i}+\tilde{\mu}_{Kt+j}{\mathbf{X}}^{*}\big)\|^{2}}{N}=0.\end{split}

(333)

Recall that (
318
) holds for
k\in[t]
by the induction hypothesis and (
299
) holds for
k\in[t+1]
,
\ell\in[K-1]
(thanks to the induction hypothesis and the argument above). Hence, by plugging in the formulas for
\{{\sf c}_{t+1,i}\}_{i\in[t+1]}
,
\mu_{t+1}
and
\{\theta_{t+1,i}\}_{i\in[K(t+1)]}
(cf. (
267
), (
265
) and (
266
)), we have

\begin{split}\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\|{\mathbf{J}}({\mathbf{Y}}){\mathbf{u}}^{t+1}-\sum_{i=1}^{t+1}{\sf c}_{t+1,i}{\mathbf{u}}^{i}-\mu_{t}{\mathbf{X}}^{*}-\sum_{i=1}^{K(t+1)}\theta_{t+1,i}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}^{i}\|^{2}}{N}=0.\end{split}

(334)

By recalling the definitions of
{\mathbf{f}}^{t+1}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{f}}^{t+1}
(cf. (
249
) and (
296
)), (
334
) implies that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\|{\mathbf{f}}^{t+1}-\tilde{\mathbf{f}}^{t+1}\|^{2}}{N}=0.

(335)

As
g_{t+2}
is Lipschitz, (
335
) also gives that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\|{\mathbf{u}}^{t+2}-\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{K(t+1)+1}\|^{2}}{N}=0.

(336)

Then, by using (
315
) with
i=t+1
and Proposition
3
, we obtain that (
319
) and (
320
) hold for
k=t+1
, thus concluding the inductive proof. The result we have just proved by induction, combined with (
297
), gives that (
295
) holds.

Another application of Proposition
3
, together with (
295
), gives that

\begin{split}\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\psi\big(&\tilde{u}^{1}_{i},\tilde{u}^{K+1}_{i},\ldots,\tilde{u}^{Kt+1}_{i},\tilde{f}^{1}_{i},\tilde{f}^{2}_{i},\ldots,\tilde{f}^{t}_{i},X^{*}_{i}\big)\\
&=\mathbb{E}[\psi(\tilde{U}_{1},\tilde{U}_{K+1},\ldots,\tilde{U}_{Kt+1},F_{1},\ldots,F_{t},X^{*})],\end{split}

(337)

where we recall that, by the definition in the theorem statement, for
s\in\{1,\ldots,t\}
,

F_{s}=\mu_{s}X^{*}+\sum_{i=1}^{Ks}\theta_{s,i}\tilde{Z}_{i}.

(338)

As
U_{s+1}=g_{s+1}(F_{s})
, we have
\tilde{U}_{Ks+1}=U_{s+1}
for all
s\in[t]
, and the proof is complete.
∎
